# Canonical CaTT terms for the cells of Gray tensors of pasting diagrams: all pairs, all cubes, iterated tensors

*A self-contained problem. It assumes familiarity with the type theory CaTT, strict ω-categories and Steiner's directed complexes, and nothing else. Everything under "What is known" is proved and is not re-asked.*

**What we are asking for.** The most general theorem: a construction for **all** pairs and all finite families of globular pasting diagrams, compatible with associativity, or a proof that none exists for some family together with the equations such a counterexample forces. CT now holds for every iterated tensor **through degree four** (item 24), for all products of globes of total dimension five, for every tensor with a path factor, with I ⊗ I or with D² (items 19–21), and for D³ ⊗ D³ (item 22). The first gap in every family is the same: choosing terms for **composite cells of dimension ≥ 5** so that their faces match literally (a correction at cut four and above). The Gray cubes I^{⊗n}, n ≥ 6, are the cleanest instance, and they are now also needed elsewhere (item 28). Results for one more case are not what we want.

## 1. Setting

**CaTT** (Finster–Mimram, LICS 2017, arXiv 1706.02866). Types are `⋆` or `u →_A v`. Terms are variables or `coh_{P, u →_A v}[σ]`, where P is a context presenting a globular pasting diagram, σ is a substitution into P, and either (coherence) `Var(u : A) = Var(P) = Var(v : A)`, or (composition) `Var(u : A) = Var(∂⁻P)` and `Var(v : A) = Var(∂⁺P)`. The type of `coh_{P, u→_A v}[σ]` is `u[σ] →_{A[σ]} v[σ]`. **Definitional equality is syntactic identity.** A context is well formed when, for each variable `x : u →_A v`, the terms u and v have type A syntactically.

**Gray tensors.** For globular pasting diagrams Θ, Δ, let λΘ, λΔ be their Steiner complexes and ν(λΘ ⊗ λΔ) the strict ω-category of the tensor (Steiner, *Omega-categories and chain complexes*, HHA 2004). Its generators are the pairs y ⊗ x of cells, of dimension dim y + dim x, with boundary given by the Leibniz formula. Its cells are molecules (Hadzihasanovic, *Combinatorics of higher-categorical diagrams*, arXiv 2404.07273) and are determined by their sets of generators.

**The family context.** Fam(Θ ⊗ Δ) has one variable v_g per generator g, typed `T(∂⁻g) → T(∂⁺g)`, where T(c) is a CaTT term over the earlier variables denoting the cell c.

## 2. Statement

> **Conjecture CT.** For all finite globular pasting diagrams Θ, Δ there is an assignment T from cells c of ν(λΘ ⊗ λΔ) of dimension ≥ 1 to CaTT terms over Fam(Θ ⊗ Δ) such that
> - (a) T(g) = v_g for every generator g;
> - (b) T(c) is built from the variables by coh terms, and its strict interpretation (v_g ↦ g) is c;
> - (c) `T(c) : T(∂⁻c) → T(∂⁺c)` holds **syntactically** for every cell c. In particular Fam(Θ ⊗ Δ) is well formed.

The same question for iterated tensors λΘ₁ ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ λΘ_k.

## 3. What is known

1. **Low dimensions.** For Θ, Δ of dimension ≤ 1, binary composites can be chosen consistently by hand. CT holds for D² ⊗ D¹.
2. **Layered terms fail for D¹ ⊗ D¹ ⊗ D¹.** "Layered" means T(c) is the vertical composite, along a layering of the molecule c, of whiskered generators. The source hexagon of the top cell has a unique layering whose first two layers are a square whiskered on the right and a square whiskered on the left, meeting at a 3-edge path. The first layer's target contains a subterm denoting the 2-edge path bc, and no subterm of the second layer's source denotes bc (in CaTT every term uses exactly the variables of its strict image). So the two sides bracket the 3-edge path differently and (c) fails. Non-layered terms are not ruled out.
3. **The weak form holds through dimension 4.** With (c′) — T(c) has a type u → v with u, v connected to T(∂⁻c), T(∂⁺c) by invertible coherence cells, inserted into the variables' types — CT holds for generators of dimension ≤ 4.
4. **Single coherence heads do not avoid the problem directly.** Taking T(c) to be one composition coherence coh_{P, u→v}[σ] requires T(∂^±c) to be literally u[σ], v[σ]; this fails when the boundary is a composite of lower cells that are not among σ's arguments.
5. **Semantics is settled.** The (∞,ω) Gray tensor of pasting diagrams is the free (∞,ω)-category on the Steiner tensor (Campion, arXiv 2311.00205, Cor. 3.5; Chanavat, arXiv 2603.22242, Thm 3.29). CT is only about syntax.
6. **Suspension closure (all dimensions).** With I = D¹ and P a finite globular pasting diagram, CT(I ⊗ P) ⇒ CT(I ⊗ ΣP). Proof: the hom (I ⊗ ΣP)(A, D) between the two extreme vertices is strictly isomorphic to I ⊗ P (checked on chains, f_r bijective on bases for r > 0, degree 0 giving the two kinds of A → D paths); every other hom is globular. Whiskerings R_r, L_r over D^r ∨₀ D¹ and D¹ ∨₀ D^r with recursively chosen types satisfy the composition-case support condition. In a globular pasting diagram every positive-dimensional generator has generators as source and target, so the endpoint declarations of Fam(I ⊗ P) have variable boundaries; a context Γ′ with a substitution σ : Γ′ → Σ_{A,D} Fam(I ⊗ P) then declares the mixed variables by σ-substituted suspended types, and T′(c) = (ΣT(c̄))[σ] satisfies (c) by stability of typing under suspension and substitution. Consequences: CT holds for I ⊗ Dⁿ, Dⁿ ⊗ I (via the odd-dimensional opposite) and I ⊗ Σ^r[m], for every n, r, m.
7. **Wedge closure for cylinders (all dimensions).** For finite globular P, Q: CT(I ⊗ P) ∧ CT(I ⊗ Q) ⇒ CT(I ⊗ (P ∨ Q)). Write z for the wedge point, u : z₀ → z₁ for the seam, a ∈ P₀∖{z}, b ∈ Q₀∖{z}, U = P(a,z), V = Q(z,b), L = (I⊗P)(a₀,z₁), R = (I⊗Q)(z₀,b₁).
   - *Strict part.* i : U → L (p ↦ p₀ # u) is a full sieve and j : V → R (q ↦ u # q₁) a full cosieve. This follows from the sign test: a nonzero positive chain of positive degree in a strongly loop-free unital basis has a differential with both signs.
   - The crossing hom (I⊗(P∨Q))(a₀,b₁) is the full sub-ω-category of L × R on the vertices (l, r) with l ∈ iU₀ or r ∈ jV₀. On Steiner tableaux the inverse is l + r − E_u, with E_u the tableau of u.
   - Every crossing cell from (ip, r) to (l, jq) factors strictly as (1_p, β) # (α, 1_q) through d = (p, q). Crucially, d depends only on the cell's 0-endpoints, so it is unchanged under higher faces.
   - *Terms.* Horizontal composites of the given terms on X = L × V and Y = U × R. One 2-coherence γ_d : S_Y(d) → S_X(d) per vertex d of U × V, over the generic path context, with identity image. Composition heads A_m (∗₀), V_m (∗₁) and W_m (∗₁ with a 2-term) whose types are prescribed recursively, so that ∂A_m(a,b) = A_{m−1}(∂a, ∂b) etc. literally.
   - The four endpoint cases are assigned S_X, S_Y, W_m(S_Y(h), γ_d) and V_m(W_m(S_Y(h_Y), γ_d), S_X(h_X)). Literal boundary agreement then holds in every dimension.
   With item 6 and the Batanin-tree decomposition P = ⋁ ΣP_j, this gives **CT(I ⊗ P) and CT(P ⊗ I) for every finite globular P**.
8. **The three-interval cube.** CT holds for I ⊗ I ⊗ I, the same terms serving both parenthesizations. Choose right-associated composites for paths. Declare each square with its two path terms. For a 2-cell (a sequence of adjacent swaps), whisker each square and pre- and post-compose with path coherences (identity image) so that consecutive steps share the same path term, then compose vertically. Declare the top cell between the two corrected full routes 321 → … → 123. All nonidentity cells (30 paths, 18 2-cells, 1 top cell) have been machine-checked against a certified CaTT checker. So the failure of *layered* terms (item 2) is a failure of that particular choice only.
9. **Where the cylinder argument stops.** For a general first factor Θ in place of I, reconstructing a cell across a wedge of the second factor subtracts a varying cell of the common copy of Θ, not the fixed seam tableau E_u. Positivity conditions then occur in degrees above one, and the intermediate vertex d of item 7 is no longer determined by the 0-endpoints, so a single 2-coherence per vertex no longer suffices.

10. **A strict comparison for the diagonal hom of two suspensions (all dimensions).** For finite globular P and Q, write ΣP and ΣQ for their suspensions, with vertices 0 and 1, and Sp for the suspended basis element of p. Let ε be the augmentation, extended by 0 in positive degree. The degree-+1 chain map
    - F(p ⊗ e ⊗ q) = Sp ⊗ Sq,
    - F(p ⊗ v ⊗ q) = ε(q) Sp ⊗ (1 − v) + ε(p) v ⊗ Sq (v = 0, 1),
    
    has nonnegative coefficients and commutes with ∂ in positive degree. Here e is the arrow of I, and at total degree 0 the image is one of the two directed paths from (0,0) to (1,1). Prefixing the row ((0,0),(1,1)) to the image of each tableau row gives a strict functor **Φ : P ⊗ I ⊗ Q → (ΣP ⊗ ΣQ)((0,0),(1,1))**. Φ sends the atom p ⊗ e ⊗ q to the atom Sp ⊗ Sq, so every mixed atom of ΣP ⊗ ΣQ has a source of one lower dimension. Φ is not injective.

11. **Φ has a pinned globular section for all finite globular P and Q.** There is a map s of underlying globular sets, commuting with both faces and preserving identities, with Φ s = id and s(Sp ⊗ Sq) = p ⊗ e ⊗ q on every mixed atom. Proof outline:
    - For two paths J = [r] and K = [s], cells of the diagonal hom are lifted directly. Arrows become sorted words (P-moves before Q-moves on each route, with the middle move at the crossing). 2-cells become sequences of oriented square swaps, whose extra squares lie in the endpoint panels and have identity image. 3-cells become whiskered oriented cubes, after strict exchange of disjoint swap blocks.
    - For general P and Q, a diagonal cell y is cut into local data over pairs (u, w) of nodes of the Batanin trees, by restriction to node paths and augmentation. Each local triple (U on J ⊗ I, V on I ⊗ K, W on J ⊗ K one degree lower), subject to three compatibility equations, is lifted by the path case. The bottom rows of the local lifts are then assembled into a tableau of P ⊗ I ⊗ Q. A coefficient bound (each prospective bottom row has mass ≤ 1, by positive chain maps to globes) makes each local bottom row a single vertex triple.
    - Restricted to the faces of the mixed atoms, Φ is injective, and the face-closure of the mixed atoms is recovered row by row from the image.
    
    The section is checked by machine on every diagonal cell of D⁴ ⊗ D⁴ and of a pair of branching trees.
12. **Transfer theorem: CT(P ⊗ I ⊗ Q) ⇒ CT(ΣP ⊗ ΣQ) for all finite globular P, Q.** Let U be a CT assignment for B = P ⊗ I ⊗ Q, and s the section of item 11. Build a substitution ρ from the family context of ΣP ⊗ ΣQ into Σ Fam(B), in order of dimension. Each endpoint-panel generator of B is sent to a boundary lift, inside the route pasting context R_v (R₀ = ΣQ ∨₀ ΣP, R₁ = ΣP ∨₀ ΣQ), of its strict image with the literally prescribed substituted endpoints. This is possible because strict interpretation on a pasting context is a trivial fibration, and the support of a term is that of its strict image. Each mixed generator Sp ⊗ Sq is declared with the ρ-substituted suspended type of p ⊗ e ⊗ q. Then T(c) = (Σ U(s c̄))[ρ] on the diagonal hom, and boundary lifting on the pure sides, satisfies (a)–(c). Composition preservation of s is not needed. For P = Q = I the panel lifts can be taken to be single exchange coherences over D² ∨₀ D²; this case was also checked by a certified CaTT checker.
13. **CT through dimension 3, and in dimension 4 when the top cells are atoms.** For any family context, choose terms degree by degree. Take the variable on a generator and the identity on an identity. For any other cell, compose the terms of a minimal strict expression, then correct its j-faces for j = 1, …, d−1 by composing at cut j with identity-image (j+1)-terms. Those terms live in the ≤ j-dimensional subcontext and exist by RC(j) there. RC(1) and RC(2) hold in every CaTT context, so this gives (a)–(c) for every cell of dimension ≤ 3. In degree 4 only declarations are needed when every non-identity 4-cell is an atom, which holds for all iterated tensors of globes of total dimension 4 (checked exhaustively). Making the choices on flattened basis tuples gives the same terms for every parenthesization. **The same argument works in every dimension given RC(j) for the j-skeleta of the family context.** These skeleta are strongly loop-free Steiner, so relative coherence on finite strong-Steiner contexts (any two parallel n-terms with the same strict image are joined by an (n+1)-term) would give CT for every tensor of globular pasting diagrams.
14. **Consequences.** CT holds for: every iterated tensor of globes of total dimension ≤ 4, including the four-cube I^{⊗4} compatibly with every parenthesization; D² ⊗ D² (with its identity towers), D³ ⊗ D² and D² ⊗ D³; and ΣJ ⊗ ΣK for all paths J = [r], K = [s] (each factor a vertical string of 2-generators of any length), since [r] ⊗ I ⊗ [s] has dimension ≤ 3.

15. **Wedge closure with a finite path factor (all dimensions).** For every finite path J = [r] and finite globular P, Q: CT(J ⊗ P) ∧ CT(J ⊗ Q) ⇒ CT(J ⊗ (P ∨₀ Q)). Write z for the common vertex. For a vertex l (a path) on the P side, let A(l) be the time of its last P-edge; for a vertex q on the Q side, let B(q) be the time of its first Q-edge.
    - The crossing hom is the full subcategory of L × R on the pairs with A(l) ≤ B(q), with inverse x + y − E_ij. Both A and B are nondecreasing along arrows.
    - Every crossing cell h factors as (1_{l₋}, β) #₀ (α, 1_{q₊}) through d = (l₋, q₊). The two factors split horizontally at the times A(l₋) and B(q₊), and all faces of h share the same d and the same two times.
    - The terms are cut-1 composition heads of horizontal composition heads, padded by path comparisons over the path contexts.
16. **A single seam path is impossible for three factors.** In I_A ⊗ I_B ⊗ [2] (spatial edges C, D), take the strict 2-cells CDBA → CDAB → CADB → ACDB → ACBD → ABCD and CDBA → CBDA → BCDA → BCAD → BACD → ABCD.
    - They are the two faces of one 3-cell: the image of the generating cube under the map that sends the last edge of I to CD.
    - The first can only factor through the seam path CABD, and the second only through CBAD, because the position in [2] of the BA → AB square is invariant.
    - The same happens in P ⊗ I ⊗ (Q ∨₀ Q′) whenever P, Q and Q′ each have an edge, via a strict retraction.

    So wedge closure with an interval in the middle cannot use one intermediate vertex per cell, as items 7 and 15 do.
17. **Cylinder factorization of crossing cells.** Let K be any finite strong-Steiner complex (for example P ⊗ I), W = U ∨_z V, and X, Y endpoints strictly on opposite sides of z. Each n-cell h of (K ⊗ W)(X, Y) factors uniquely as a normalized strict map D^n ⊗ [3] → K ⊗ W, with constant ends at X and Y and three cylinders in K ⊗ U, K ⊗ {z} and K ⊗ V.
    - The columns are L(g), Z(g), R(g): the parts of each signed row off the seam in U, at z, and off the seam in V. The moving vertices A(g) and B(g) are the incoming and outgoing edge sums at z.
    - The factorization commutes with faces, identities and hom composition.
    - Two projected cells l and r glue (as c = l + r − θ_z) **iff the middle column is a directed cylinder** D^n ⊗ I → K. Every positive degree can be the first degree in which this fails, so no finite set of low rows decides it.
18. **Suspension in any slot is a pullback.** (A ⊗ ΣP ⊗ B)((x,0,b), (y,1,b′)) ≅ (A ⊗ I ⊗ B)(X, Y) ×_{A ⊗ B′} (A ⊗ P ⊗ B′), where B′ is B with its differential negated in positive degrees, the right map augments the middle factor, and the left map takes the interval-edge column.

19. **Path factors (all dimensions).** CT([r] ⊗ P) and CT(P ⊗ [r]) for every finite path [r] and every finite globular P, with the ordinary cylinder declarations on every generator e ⊗ g. A crossing cell over s path edges is a stack of 2s + 1 strict cylinders (layer, edge, layer, …). Each is lifted in a globular context with prescribed caps and sides (boundary lifting on pasting contexts), the stack is formed by weak cylinder stacking (BMOSV, arXiv 2501.11620, Thm 4.3), and the two constant ends are normalized by an operation N_m that pads with whiskered identities. N_m only uses comparisons over generic pasting contexts, so no relative coherence in the ambient context is needed. This settles the constant-end part of the cylinder-lifting problem for arbitrary K.
20. **Wedge closure with a low-dimensional seam.** For every finite strong-Steiner K of dimension ≤ 2 and finite globular U, V: CT(K ⊗ U) ∧ CT(K ⊗ V) ⇒ CT(K ⊗ (U ∨₀ V)). The proof lifts the three strict cylinders of item 17 with caps fixed in the seam. It corrects at cuts 1, …, d with RC(1), RC(2) (d = dim K ≤ 2), and above disk degree d with the identity-cap comparisons of item 19. For dim K ≥ 3 the same lemma needs comparisons of parallel j-terms for j ≤ dim K.
21. **Polygons and D².** CT(B_{r,s} ⊗ P) for all r, s ≥ 2 and all P, where B_{r,s} is one 2-generator between two paths of lengths r and s. Hence CT(I ⊗ I ⊗ P), CT(D² ⊗ P) and the reverse orders, for every P. The suspension step uses a strict comparison Ψ : H ⊗ P → (B ⊗ ΣP)((x,0),(y,1)) with H the diagonal hom of B ⊗ I, a pinned section built from first-edge and last-edge collapses, and wedges by item 20. The same induction does not repeat with seam D² ⊗ I: its diagonal hom has a commuting square of indecomposable moves, so its underlying 1-category is not free.
22. **D³ ⊗ D³.** For every a, b ≥ 0 there is a strict comparison D^a ⊗ B_{3,3} ⊗ D^b → (D^{a+1} ⊗ I ⊗ D^{b+1})(bottom, top) with a pinned globular section (B_{3,3} the directed hexagon of traversal orders). The CaTT transfer is done for a = b = 1, giving CT(D² ⊗ I ⊗ D²) and, by item 12, CT(D³ ⊗ D³).
23. **The permutohedron route of the previous round is limited.** The comparison from a permutohedral shape to the diagonal hom of ΣP₁ ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ ΣP_k has a pinned section for the four-cube, but for five axes an explicit 3-cell has no lift under the natural orientation, and for six or more axes no interchange orientation depending only on the swapping pair and its block order makes the trace surjective. Other sources of terms are not excluded.
24. **CT through degree four for every iterated tensor; dimension five for products of globes.** Choose terms through dimension three as interpretations of biased tricategorical expressions. For a 3-presentation with acyclic generating 1-graph and nonempty boundary paths of 2-generators, the free tricategory is faithful over the free strict 3-category on parallel 3-cells (the thin interchanger subgroupoid on the words of a strict 2-cell is connected with unique arrows; Guthmann's strictification, arXiv 1903.05777). CaTT 3-terms modulo 4-terms in both directions interpret the tricategory operations, so each needed equality gives literal CaTT 4-comparisons at cut three. This gives a CT assignment through degree four, composite 4-cells included, for every iterated Gray tensor of finite globular pasting diagrams, compatibly with every parenthesization. A product of globes of total dimension d has exactly one nonidentity d-cell (its top atom), so CT holds for every product of globes of total dimension five, the five-cube included. **Composite 5-cells need a correction at cut four, which neither argument supplies.**
25. **Globe pairs in arbitrary dimensions (one uniform assignment).** For D^m ⊗ D^n, all m, n: CT on the subpresentation generated by the bidegrees (i, j) with i ≤ 2 or j ≤ 2, including cells containing both (2,3) and (3,2). A section extends when either globe grows. Adjoining (3,3) requires the atom and 120 padded single-generator shapes (s_i s_j inputs for bidegree (i, j), s the little Schröder numbers 1, 3, 11, 45, …). Twenty-five shapes are done, all 121 central singletons lift with one common choice of five-faces, and compatibility with the rest of the section is outstanding on exactly 192 five-face shapes. Moreover all 121 central singletons lift with five-dimensional boundaries that are words in **one** family of elementary terms: pure moves are depth-zero actions in every dimension, every exchange of a pure move with an independent move is the naturality operation applied to the other move, and two smaller mixed exchanges (index pairs (1,3) and (2,4)) are proved in D³ ⊗ D³. The usual lexicographic word works on 112 inputs; a single amended rule (add the precedence 4 ≺ 1 when 2 ≺ 1, 2 ≺ 3 and 4 ≺ 3) covers all 121 by changing nine negative faces. Keeping the lexicographic word everywhere instead needs one more mixed exchange, index pair (1,4), on a two-event tableau in D⁴ ⊗ D⁴ with top labels (A_{1,+}, B_{4,*}) and (A_{4,*}, B_{1,+}), and with the four elementary 5-terms already fixed. These lexicographic five-terms have not been identified with the rest of the width-two assignment. In degree six (one more round of the same method): a word construction with two matchings W₀, W₁ needs only short lifts plus comparisons W₀(z) ≅ W₁(z) on single runs that change direction (2, 10, 40, 156 shapes in degrees 2–5); both signs of the (1,3) and (2,4) mixed exchanges exist; and the whole degree-six layer for all globe pairs needs, besides short tests at (2,4) and (4,2), exactly two signed distant exchanges in D⁴ ⊗ D⁴, which this method cannot avoid. **For D³ ⊗ D⁴, CT is reduced to two explicit 6-terms** (the short (2,4) lifts with stationary (3,1) padding, of type W(∂⁻w) → W(∂⁺w)); everything else, including the top 7-atom, is assembled and machine-checked from them. So the globe-pair route is advancing one bidegree per round, which is why we are asking for a uniform argument instead (Q1–Q2). Any two paths of elementary changes between serializations of the same 3-cell admit a CaTT 5-comparison with exact endpoints.
26. **Reduction of route (iii).** For the relative-coherence route it suffices, in every iterated tensor, to compare parallel terms whose strict top chain is a single generator with coefficient one (lower padding allowed). On finite strong-Steiner presentations, full relative coherence is equivalent to comparing parallel (k+1)-terms over the first identity on each intrinsic k-cell of the k-skeleton, for all k ≥ 1.
27. **One suspended factor.** D^m ⊗ D^n lifts strictly into the diagonal hom of D^{m+1} ⊗ D^n, pinning every generator of maximal right rank; every diagonal cell is in such a lift on a face, adds one pure right factor to it, or lies on a horizontal wedge of pure globes. A typed substitution realizes the lift on all pure variables and all variables of right rank one, in every dimension. The remaining substitution problem is explicit.
28. **Why the cubes matter now.** In the companion problem on relative coherence, a CaTT presentation of the Gray m-cube whose face subcontexts present the faces (CT for I^{⊗m}) is exactly what turns an (m−1)-sphere in a geometric fibre of a "seam" computad into a failure of relative coherence. CT for m ≤ 5 decides those computads with seam cycles of length ≤ 5. The first undecided one needs the six-cube. That case has since been pushed down to three dimensions without the six-cube (relative coherence r6, Response 19), but CT for all cubes would still decide every seam presentation at once.

## 4. Questions

**Q1 (main).** Decide CT for **all** pairs Θ, Δ of finite globular pasting diagrams and for all iterated tensors Θ₁ ⊗ ⋯ ⊗ Θ_k, compatibly with associativity. By item 24 everything through degree four is done, so the question is about composite cells of dimension ≥ 5. A positive answer may use any terms. A negative answer should give a family with a proof that no assignment satisfies (a)–(c).

Natural routes:
- (i) **Higher-categorical coherence one level up.** Item 24 chooses 3-dimensional terms from tricategorical operations, so that faithfulness of free tricategories over strict 3-categories supplies the 4-comparisons. A version for degree n would choose (n−1)-dimensional terms from operations of a free algebraic (n−1)- or n-category (for example a Batanin–Leinster or Trimble-style operad, or Gray-enriched iterations) and needs **faithfulness of the free weak structure over the strict one on parallel top cells** for the presentations that occur (strong-Steiner skeleta of Gray tensors). Prove this faithfulness, or find where it fails.
- (ii) **Relative coherence on strong-Steiner skeleta.** By item 13 and item 26, CT follows from identity-image comparisons between parallel terms whose strict top chain is a single generator, on the skeleta of the family contexts. Prove this. (The companion relative-coherence problem shows that such comparisons can fail on positive non-Steiner computads, so the Steiner hypothesis must be used.)
- (iii) **Cylinder lifting in general layers.** Items 17, 19 and 20 reduce a three-factor tensor to lifting strict cylinders with shared caps. Item 20 does this when the seam has dimension ≤ 2. Extend it to seams of every dimension, for example K = P ⊗ I.

**Q2 (fallback, still every dimension).** If Q1 is out of reach, prove CT for **every Gray cube I^{⊗n}** (all n, compatibly with every parenthesization), or for every pair D^m ⊗ D^n of globes, by a construction uniform in the dimensions. For the cubes, a CaTT presentation of the n-cube in which each face subcontext presents that face is what item 28 needs.

**Q3 (if CT fails).** If CT fails for some pair or family, prove that no assignment satisfies (a)–(c) and identify the minimal extra rule that makes the family contexts well formed (for example a typing rule for generators whose boundaries are molecules, or a definitional equation between bracketings). A precise description of the forced equations is the useful answer.

## 5. What counts as a solution

An explicit construction of T for all pairs and iterated tensors; CT for all cubes or all pairs of globes by a uniform construction; faithfulness of free weak n-categories over strict ones on the relevant presentations, or relative coherence on strong-Steiner skeleta, in every dimension; an impossibility proof with the forced equations; or a published reference.

## 6. Literature (searched 2026-11-18 and 2026-11-19; references added 2026-11-20 from the round-6 answers)

- Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, arXiv 2501.11620, §4, Thm 4.3 (weak cylinder stacking).

- Steiner, *Simple omega-categories and chain complexes*, arXiv math/0403237 (tableaux and the tensor product, used for items 10–12).
- Steiner, HHA 2004; Hadzihasanovic, arXiv 2404.07273 (molecules, layerings, Prop. 8.3.17 on supports).
- Campion, arXiv 2311.00205; Chanavat, arXiv 2603.22242.
- Finster–Mimram, arXiv 1706.02866; Benjamin–Markakis, arXiv 2406.12127 (invertibility of coherences).
- No paper on syntactically matched terms for non-globular molecules in a type theory without definitional equalities was found.
- Guthmann, *Strictification of tricategories*, arXiv 1903.05777 (§§3.5, 3.8; used in item 24).
- Di Vittorio, *A Gray-categorical pasting theorem*, arXiv 2205.15486; Griffiths, arXiv 2101.00077 (the tricategory operad, Def. 9.4).
- Steiner, *Simple omega-categories and chain complexes*, arXiv math/0403237 (tableaux; used throughout items 19–25).


# Researcher Response 1

**A full diagonal row of a globe pair admits a cubical presentation with a pinned globular section, uniformly in the width and both globe dimensions.** The projection is strict. Its section preserves both faces, identities and every cubical atom whose moving positions form an interval. In particular there is a single cubical substitution problem for the whole row, including its highest composites. A separate CaTT calculation below shows how two native assignments can supply the noninvertible faces of that substitution in width three. The outstanding conditions in that calculation are invertible comparisons over the five-skeleton.

### The full row and its cubical projection

Write $Q_h=I^{\otimes h}$. Let $0\le h\le\min(m,n)$, $k=m+n-h$, and $l=m-h$. In $D^m\otimes D^n$ take all cells whose degree-$k$ row contains the $h+1$ sites
\[
                      (l+b,n-b),\qquad 0\le b\le h .
\]
They share both poles below degree $k$. Indeed, the rank ideal contains the entire truncated rectangle through $k$. Every available successor below the cut occurs, so the signed tags there are those of the top atom by the peak formula. Regard a $k$-cell as an object and cut-$k$ composition as composition of arrows. Write $C_h$ for this strict iterated hom with its full row. The formulas below identify it independently of $m,n$, with a parity change in the decoding of the second tag. For $h=0$ it is a point.

Number the positions $1,\ldots,h$ in **decreasing Gray tensor order**. A cube vertex $z$ is a bit string. Send it to the displayed $k$-row by giving site $b$ first tag $z_{b+1}$ and second tag $((l+b)\bmod 2)\mathbin{\mathrm{XOR}}z_b$. At $b=h$ the first tag is $*$, and at $b=0$ the second tag is $*$. These are all the objects, since adjacent sites have exactly one free notch bit.

For a positive cubical basis face with moving set $S$, give the top-chain map value zero when $S$ is disconnected. When $S=[a,b]$ give it the single label at
\[
             (l+b,n-a+1).
\]
Its first tag is $z_{b+1}$, its second is $((l+a-1)\bmod 2)\mathbin{\mathrm{XOR}}z_{a-1}$, with the same star conventions at the two exterior bounds. Thus a surviving label can be recorded as an interval with the fixed bits immediately to its left and right. In degree zero one uses the whole vertex row defined above.

These formulas commute with the differential in positive degrees. On an interval of length at least two only the two end deletions survive. Deleting $b$ has sign $+$ in decreasing tensor order and changes the first tag on $[a,b-1]$. Deleting $a$ has sign $(-1)^{b-a}$ and the decoding on $[a+1,b]$ contributes $(-1)^{l+a}$. Their product is the required second-coordinate sign $(-1)^{l+b}$. For a singleton move at $b$, only the two vertex entries at $b-1,b$ change, by exactly these horizontal and vertical increments. For a disconnected set any surviving deletion removes an isolated position separated from the surviving interval by a gap. The two choices of bit there have equal images and cancel. The differential of every vertex row is the common pole difference. The map is positive, so applying it to the rows and prefixing the fixed pole rows defines a strict functor $F_h:Q_h\to C_h$. Composition follows directly by addition at the cut. A face with zero top chain can still have a nontrivial image in lower rows.

### A path in the cube for every cube of joins

There is a strict functor
\[
            P_h:Q_{h-1}\longrightarrow Q_h(0^h,1^h),\qquad h\ge1 .
\]
Its input positions $1,\ldots,h-1$ are joins between successive output positions. A zero join separates two components. In each component a one join puts its positions in the same block and a moving join separates two blocks. For a vertex, read components left to right and execute the positions of each block in decreasing order. This gives an actual path from $0^h$ to $1^h$.

Define the degree-raising linear map on each higher face as follows. If moving joins occur in two or more components, give it zero. Otherwise let the active component have blocks $B_0,\ldots,B_r$ in increasing positional order. Take the tensor of their decreasing paths in Gray order $B_r,\ldots,B_0$. This chain of degree $r+1$ contains one moving position from each block in every summand. Within a block all larger positions are already one and all smaller ones are zero. Freeze earlier components at their terminal vertices and later components at their initial vertices.

For the differential calculation write $A_j$ for the path of block $B_j$. The face setting join $j$ to one merges $A_j,A_{j-1}$ in that temporal order. Its new path chain is $A_j\otimes 0_{j-1}+1_j\otimes A_{j-1}$, with the remaining factors unchanged. In the cubical boundary it has sign $(-1)^{r-j}$. Consequently the term with $0_{j-1}$ has the negative boundary sign of block $j-1$ and the term with $1_j$ has the positive boundary sign of block $j$. Splitting an interior join at zero leaves two active components and contributes zero. The two exterior splits supply the missing $1_0$ and $0_r$ terms. For $r=1$ these are the two summands of the single split path. This is exactly the Leibniz differential of the tensor of paths. When multiple components are active, a surviving facet must fix the sole moving join of one component. Its two signs freeze that component at the same extreme vertex as seen from the other active component, and cancel. This proves the functor assertion after prefixing the common endpoints. On the top atom $P_h$ gives the top atom of $Q_h$.

The relation enabling a section is
\[
     C_h(0^h,1^h)\ \cong\ C_{h-1},
       \qquad F_h P_h=F_{h-1}\quad\text{under this identification}.
\]
Here $F_h$ on a diagonal cell means its induced map of homs. In $C_h$ the bit at $i$ changes exactly when its degree-one site occurs. The diagonal hom therefore has the full degree-$(k+1)$ row, precisely the next full-row corner in the same rectangle. In interval coordinates the object bits on this next row are the right tags of the first $h-1$ singleton intervals. A higher interval $[a,b]$ becomes $[a,b-1]$; at the left end the stored bit is complemented because $l$ increases by one.

On a join vertex in $P_h$, position $i+1$ has already executed when position $i$ executes exactly when their join is one. This recovers all the stated object bits. For a higher face, choosing one position per block can produce a contiguous physical interval only if all interior blocks are singletons. Equivalently, the moving joins form one contiguous interval. Then there is exactly one such summand. It uses the greatest position in its first block and the least in its last. Immediately to its right the fixed physical bit equals the adjacent join bit. Immediately to its left it equals the complement of that join bit, since a joined predecessor executes later and a separate preceding component has already finished. The change of $l$ cancels this complement. This proves the equation on every cubical basis face, including the disconnected cases.

### Construction of the pinned section

Induct on $h$. Lift each object to its bit vertex. For a positive cell $c$ its two object boundaries $s,t$ differ on a nonempty set $M$, always from zero to one. A higher interval label uses only moving positions: all singleton intervals beneath it occur by downward closure. If $M$ is the whole set, regard $c$ as $c'$ in $C_{h-1}$ by the diagonal identification and define
\[
                     \sigma_h(c)=P_h(\sigma_{h-1}(c')).
\]
Use the point section to start the recursion. The intertwining identity proves $F_h\sigma_h(c)=c$.

Otherwise split $M$ into its maximal consecutive runs in increasing order. Each positive row splits by those runs, with no precedence between them at cut $k$. From one run $[a,b]$ extract an element of $C_{b-a+1}$ by restricting its vertex bits, translating its interval indices and dropping the exterior tags at the new bounds. Here is also a direct validity check for the restriction. The cut formula splits off the run as a positive tableau; different runs have disjoint degree-one positions with a fixed gap. In the run factor, any immediate exterior bit used by a positive interval is constant. Relabelling that bit by a star preserves the interval differential: internal end deletions still change their indicated tags, and the deleted exterior bit is never changed by a move. At the bottom the retained bit increments are exactly those in the restricted vertex. Decoding with the new offset gives the tableau equations, positivity and the common poles in the smaller rectangle. Conversely, reinsert the fixed exterior bits to recover the entire factor.

Apply the smaller section in each run. Place its cube in the actual coordinate face with all earlier runs finished and all later runs unstarted. Compose these lifted cells in that order at cut zero, using identities to equalize heights. The projected composite recovers the original signed rows, including the two boundary vertices. Above dimension one both faces retain the same object boundaries and hence the same run decomposition; the factors truncate or become identities. In dimension one the composite has exactly the specified bit vertices. The diagonal branch has the same face equations by functoriality of $P_h$ and the induction. Extend by ordinary identities. This proves a section of underlying globular sets in every dimension.

For a cube atom moving on a consecutive interval, the restriction to its moving run is the top atom of that run's corner. The diagonal recursion lifts that top atom to the top cube at each stage. Reinstalling its fixed coordinates therefore returns the original cube face itself. Vertices are pinned as well. There is in general no strict functor section: the two orders of distant singleton moves agree in $C_h$, while their uniquely lifted elementary edges give distinct cube paths.

The independent strict enumeration for width five found 903 full-row cells. The checker tested the section against the entire source five-cube, including its face and pin equations. In width six it constructed all 4,279 lifts and checked all 385 vertex and interval-atom pins. It also tests both chain-map differentials and the intertwining equation directly on basis faces. [Cubical corner and section checker](../../../tools/gray_globe_cubical_corner.py)

### What transfers to CaTT

Fix terms for the common lower poles. Suppose $Q_h$ has a CT assignment $U$, and a substitution realizes its $k$-fold suspended generator declarations at those poles with the strict values specified by $F_h$. Use the substituted suspended term $U(\sigma_h c)$ on each cell of the full row. Its type has the required literal boundaries by substitution and the two face equations for $\sigma_h$. Its strict value is $c$. The only globe-pair generator whose rectangle contains the full row is the top $(m,n)$ generator, since that row reaches both projection bounds. Declare it by the substituted type of the top cube variable and use that variable's image as its pin. The construction matches any assignment on the complement with the stated common lower terms and declarations. A cell with a full-row face at height at least $k$ itself has a full row. Thus no face correction from the complement is needed above the fixed poles. This is a uniform sufficient **syntactic** test. Building its substitution still requires actual terms on disconnected moving sets, whose strict top coefficients vanish. Strict cubical retractions for suspended cubes and globular pasting diagrams are also known; here the required additional datum is the substitution into the particular globe-pair family context. [Campion, Theorems 2.1–2.2 and Corollary 2.4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2209.09376)

In width three there is a more explicit gluing test in every pair $m,n\ge3$ at $k=m+n-3$. The eight objects form a bit cube. Call the three degree-one directions $A,B,C$. Suppose one native assignment supplies the two directed $AB$ squares at the two values of $C$, and another supplies the two directed $BC$ squares at the two values of $A$, each at its own two-letter boundaries. Use these names in the shifted hom, so that an edge is an actual $(k+1)$-term. Assume the two writings share their lower terms and have been placed over common generator declarations. To use the first writing $U$ for the eight objects, it suffices to give four comparisons
\[
             U(B) *_k K_t\ \Longrightarrow\ K_s *_k V(B)
\]
where $K_x:U(x)\to V(x)$ is invertible, the comparison is invertible, and their strict images are identities on the corresponding states or steps. There is no prior compatibility to check between the corner changes of different $B$ edges: those four edges partition the eight vertices. Keep $U(A),U(B)$ and transport $V(C)$ to $K_s *_k V(C) *_k K_t^{-1}$. Transport the two $BC$ squares in the same way and use the four comparisons to replace the transported $B$ edges by $U(B)$. At each interior vertex the same inverse pair cancels; reassociation and the inverse laws supply the literal outer boundaries. Now only the two $AC$ exchanges, at the two values of $B$, are needed. They must use these specified $A,C$ terms. The four adjacent squares and these two exchanges supply all the square variables of the suspended three-cube. The three-cube assignment of item 8 supplies its corrected paths and top facets. Its top variable can then be the declared $(m,n)$ atom. This gives the whole full row by the section above.

For the inputs at the end of item 25 the four middle-step comparisons can actually be constructed. The details below specify which two $AC$ exchanges remain if both competing noninvertible attachments are taken from their native constructions.

* In the signed rank-three rectangles use the right-first strip $B$ as the primary choice through degree three and on the critical four-rows. The two-strip gluing allows this choice: take the inverses of its changes of three-states and four-corners when transporting the other strip. On a critical native 5-step of site $(2,3)$ use the right-first term itself. On $(3,2)$ use the other native term with the prescribed corrections. This is the choice-of-primary-section argument of the two-strip gluing. [Round 6, Responses 14–16](../answered/canonical-gray-terms-r6-2026-11-20.md#researcher-response-14)
* For $U$ take the right-first $D^2\otimes D^3$ section, grow the right pure four-site and right edge site, choose the two-letter five-words and declare the $(2,4)$ variable. This completes $D^2\otimes D^4$, whose only 6-cell is the atom. Grow the left pure three-site and then the left edge $(3,1)$. For $V$ take the simultaneous rank-three rectangle just specified with its word faces and $(3,3)$ variable, then grow the right pure four-site and the edge $(1,4)$. The choices through degree three are the same right-first choices. The shared $(2,3)$ declaration is native and the $(1,4)$ declaration lies on the common right edge tile. On the left edge steps still inside the rank-three rectangle, $U$ can retain the right-first choices: these are available lifts with the same three-ends during its left-edge attachment. Outside the full four-row one may use $U$ if $(3,1)$ or $(2,2)$ is absent, and $V$ otherwise. The former condition excludes $(3,2)$, and the latter, outside the full row, excludes $(2,4)$. The rule is constant on faces down to degree four.
* Each short $(2,4)$ input in the full four-row is, in $U$, the native variable whiskered at cut two by a stationary pure left 3-step $X$, then at cut three by a stationary edge 4-step $e$. Use the forced spatial orders. Its two faces distribute to the actual $U$ two-step words. The inner distributor is on a cut-four string of two 5-globes attached to a 3-globe at cut two. Whisker it by $e$ and use the outer distributor on a two-step string attached to a 4-globe at cut three. Pre- and postcompose with these changes in the required directions. The checker constructs both complete 6-terms at those word boundaries. In $V$ the short $(3,3)$ lifts are the pure extensions of item 25.
* For a full middle step let $G$ be the genuine $(2,3)$ variable. Peeling $e$ and $X$ in $U$ leaves a cut-three composite of $G$ and a right pure 4-step $Y_0$. Distribute the cut-two whisker by $X$ across this composite. On the resulting pure step use the actual depth-zero action on the cut-two three-term with stationary slot $X$. It lifts to the same arity with the slot for $Y_0$ raised, by the marked-leaf substitution rule. This gives a square between the raw and distributed five-terms. On the two four-faces of the $G$ slot first choose the 5-comparisons on the reduced arity; then the two paths consisting of a five-term and the appropriate endpoint comparison lift to the full arity, giving the 6-comparison. Whisker that square by $e$, distributing the whisker over the cut-four composites on its two sides.
* In $V$ the right pure step is peeled first, then $e$, leaving the very same cut-two whiskering of $G$ by $X$. For two of the four middle steps the three spatial factors have the same order, and reassociation on their three-slot arity finishes the comparison. In the other two the stationary $(3,1)$ and $(0,4)$ factors exchange on the same side of the moving factor. Their strict two-step subcell is identical in the two orders. The pure/mixed exchange from item 25 applies to the actual $(3,1)$ step in the marked right three-variable. It is the same stationary comparison on both faces of the whiskered $G$. Moving that comparison past the whiskered $G$ is the exchange on $D^5\vee_3D^5$. Reassociate before and after, and paste with the preceding distributor square. All four results lie in the five-skeleton and hence are invertible. Their four-corner changes also lie in the four-skeleton.

This supplies the two $(2,4)$ lifts in the $U$ convention **and** their shared middle-step comparisons to $V$. It reduces the three-by-four test to two identity-image exchanges between $U(A)$ and the transported $V(C)$ over the five-skeleton. The exchanges of item 25 used a freely chosen exterior term, so they cannot be substituted here without comparing to $U(A)$. Agda checked the two native lifts and all four pasted middle-step judgments, with parameters for the existing native terms and the known stationary exchanges. The checker evaluated their full strict tableaux and verified that the eight actual four-corners occur exactly once among the four comparisons. [Middle-step checker and certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_middle_steps.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_cubical_corner.py 5 5 5 --exhaustive-cube
python3 tools/gray_globe_cubical_corner.py 6 6 6
python3 tools/gray_globe_middle_steps.py --agda /tmp/GrayMiddleSteps.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayMiddleSteps.agda
~~~

The pinned section resolves the strict lifting problem for all these corners. A construction of the cubical substitutions for arbitrary widths, or a CT assignment on every cube or every globe pair, is still missing.

# Researcher Response 2

Two boundary-choice questions about the projection $F_h:Q_h\to C_h$ of Response 1 have exact answers. A strict 2-cell lifts between specified cube paths precisely when their inversion sets are nested. A whole coordinate face admits a strict realization in a single globular pasting diagram precisely when its moving positions are pairwise nonconsecutive. On each face satisfying the second criterion, prescribed cubical declarations can be realized in CaTT once the individual spatial slots have well-typed lifts. Both statements allow arbitrary width and arbitrary frozen bits.

### Prescribing both paths of a lifted 2-cell

Let $z:s\Rightarrow t$ be a strict 2-cell of $C_h$, including an identity. Let $p,q$ be cube paths with $F_hp=s$ and $F_hq=t$. Their object endpoints agree because $F_h$ is bijective on objects. Write each path as the word of positions it changes. The words permute the same set $M$. Define $I(w)$ to consist of the pairs $(a,b)$ with $a<b$ and $b$ preceding $a$ in $w$. Then
\[
 \exists\, u:p\Rightarrow q\text{ in }Q_h,\quad F_hu=z
 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad I(p)\subseteq I(q).
\]
The decreasing tensor order of Response 1 fixes the direction of this inclusion.

Indeed, a whiskered generating square swaps two successive letters $a,b$ from increasing to decreasing order. It adds just $(a,b)$ to the inversion set. Every 2-cell is a vertical sequence of these swaps, by expanding strict composites and using interchange. This proves necessity. In the projection, a swap is visible precisely when $b=a+1$ in the original numbering. Other swaps have identity image. For fixed initial and final bits, a projected path is determined by which neighboring positions occur in inverted order. In fact the singleton edge at $i$ records exactly the bits at $i-1,i+1$ when $i$ executes, with constant tags for frozen neighbors. This accounts for the whole path tableau.

For sufficiency take a serialization of the lift $\sigma_h(z)$ of Response 1. Record, in order, only its visible crossings. Start a new serialization at $p$. Just before its next requested crossing $(a,a+1)$, let $J$ be the current inversion set. Replace $J$ by the transitive closure $J'$ of $J\cup\{(a,a+1)\}$. This is an inversion set. To see this directly, inversion sets on a finite ordered alphabet are precisely the transitive sets satisfying
\[
 (x,y)\in J,\ x<c<y \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 (x,c)\in J\ \text{or}\ (c,y)\in J
\]
for letters in that alphabet. These two conditions exclude the two possible oriented triangles in the total comparison of letters. In an increasing chain giving $(x,y)$ in the closure, an intermediate $c$ is either on the chain or straddled by an old link. Splitting that link by the displayed property proves the same property for the closure. The added link has no intermediate letter. Also $J'\subseteq I(q)$ because $I(q)$ contains the requested crossing and is transitive. The only new inversion between consecutive original positions is $(a,a+1)$.

Pass from the current word to the word for $J'$ by successive swaps. For two words with nested inversion sets, swap any successive letters out of order in the destination word. They must occur in increasing numerical order in the current word, and the swap reduces the distance to the destination by one. Thus exactly one step in this segment is visible. Its entire projected square agrees with the requested one. The projected path immediately before the crossing has the same adjacent inversions as the corresponding path in the original serialization. At the swap, $a,a+1$ are successive letters, so the bit at $a-1$ is determined by its order relative to $a$, and the bit at $a+2$ by its order relative to $a+1$. Frozen neighbors give their fixed bits. These are both exterior tags of the interval $[a,a+1]$. The boundary paths and the single top label determine the projected tableau. Repeat for every requested crossing, then sort to $q$. The last segment has no visible crossing since the projected path already equals $t$. This also proves sufficiency when $z$ is an identity.

For example the paths $13$ and $31$ across the face with moving set $\{1,3\}$ have the same image. The identity on that image lifts from $13$ to $31$. In the reverse direction the boundary alone prevents a lift. More generally the criterion depends only on $p,q$ once their images are prescribed. If one 2-cell between those projected paths lifts at $p,q$, every such 2-cell does.

### Which faces fit in one globular pasting diagram?

Fix $k=m+n-h$ as in Response 1, with $h\ge1$. By a realization in one pasting diagram we mean that the shifted diagram on a cube face factors strictly through a $k$-fold hom at common poles in a globular pasting diagram $P$, followed by a strict map $P\to D^m\otimes D^n$. The exact same criterion holds if only the vertices and generating edges have to lift with matching endpoints in that hom.

**Construction.** Let $M$ be the moving positions, with no two consecutive. The projected arrow $w$ from the initial to the terminal vertex is independent of the order of its moves. Its degree-$(k+1)$ labels are the singleton intervals at $i\in M$. At cut $k-1$, group the degree-$k$ sites $i-1,i$ with the label above them, for each $i\in M$. Every remaining degree-$k$ site forms a stationary group. These groups are disjoint. The cut precedence graph comes from the path of degree-$k$ sites by contracting the disjoint pairs. Between consecutive groups the shared cut label determines an order, the same on either face of $w$, since the bit between the groups is frozen. Choose a topological order. Starting with the negative cut row, for each successive group add the differential of its negative degree-$k$ chain to obtain the next cut row. The shared label is available when its group executes by the precedence order. The two degree-$k$ chains of an active group have equal differential by $\partial^2=0$ on its top label. Restriction of the higher rows therefore gives composable positive factors with these very cut rows. This is the ordered cut formula, applied once to $w$. [Round 6, Response 8](../answered/canonical-gray-terms-r6-2026-11-20.md#researcher-response-8)

Take the cut-$(k-1)$ wedge of one globe for each factor, of dimension $k+1$ for an active pair and $k$ for a stationary site. Send the top of each globe to its factor of $w$. For a vertex of the cube face select the indicated $k$-face in each active slot. For an edge raise its changing slot to the $(k+1)$-globe and freeze the others. Take their spatial composite at cut $k-1$. This recovers the signed rectangle rows. Changing $i$ affects just the two sites in its group. Its own upper label retains both exterior bits, since neither neighbor changes. All routes across any subface give the same arrow in the arity by strict interchange in distinct slots. Sending higher cells to iterated identities on that arrow consequently gives a strict functor. In the rectangle its value is exactly $F_h$ on the subface, since every subset of $M$ of size at least two has zero top image. The construction covers all subfaces with the same choice of slots.

**Obstruction.** Suppose instead that $a,a+1$ both move. Freeze the other positions. The two middle vertices of the resulting square can be distinguished even after projection to $C_h$. There is a strict map $d:C_h\to Q_2$ forgetting all but these two coordinates. On an object retain the two bits. On a positive interval retain its top label only when the interval is contained in $\{a,a+1\}$, using the neighboring bit inside this pair for a singleton. To check descent, ordinary cubical projection $Q_h\to Q_2$ augments all other factors. On each basis face it is the composite of the chain formula for $F_h$ with the displayed rule. This gives the rule row by row on all of $C_h$, since $F_h$ has a section. It commutes with faces and row addition at a cut. On the chosen square the composite recovers the ordinary Gray square, including its distinct two-step paths through $10$ and $01$.

In every nonempty iterated hom of a globular pasting diagram there is a compositional choice of one arrow between each reachable pair of objects. In the diagram itself the objects form a finite directed string. Pick an edge for each successive pair and concatenate. For the hom assertion, write the diagram as a wedge of suspensions along the string. The hom across a segment is the cartesian product of the suspended factors' interiors on that segment, by unique decomposition at the intervening vertices. An endomorphism hom there is terminal. Iterating takes homs coordinatewise, so the nonempty result is a product of such diagrams and the same choices work coordinatewise.

If the four vertices and four edges lifted together, use these chosen arrows in the intervening hom on both routes across the square. Their composites agree by construction. Their images in the rectangle are arrows between full-row objects, hence belong to $C_h$. Applying $d$ gives equal paths in $Q_2$ from $00$ to $11$, one factoring through $10$ and one through $01$. In the free path category of the square those paths are distinct. This contradiction already uses only the four edges. It excludes a single common arity with matching representatives there, while allowing constructions that change arities or insert comparisons after substitution.

**CaTT consequence.** For a matching $M$, assume the ordered factors of $w$ have terms with literally matching cut-$(k-1)$ faces. They give a substitution out of the globe wedge above by specifying its maximal variables. Work first in that pasting context. A prescribed CT family context on the source face, suspended $k$ times, can be instantiated there in declaration order. For a vertex use the spatial composite. For every subsequent generator the substituted source and target are parallel by typing and have the strict faces of the cell just constructed. Boundary lifting in a globular pasting context gives a term at those exact ends with that strict value. At dimension at least $k+2$ the strict value is an identity. Compose the two substitutions to obtain the terms in the rectangle. This handles even source declarations whose endpoints are two different syntactic words for the same projected arrow. It does not require relative coherence in the rectangle. In particular the separated positions $A,C$ in width three admit this construction with the edges furnished by their own spatial chart. Using the prescribed $U(A)$ and transported $V(C)$ of Response 1 would additionally require comparisons with those specific edges.

The path checker enumerated the strict cube independently by closing padded atoms under composition. In width five it tested every lifting problem for all 762 projected cells through degree two, constructing a witness with the exact faces and image on 4,780 prescribed pairs and finding no witness on the other 3,448. The spatial checker compared all 812 subface incidences in the 164 matching charts of the width-five full row with the original signed tableaux. It also checked the two-coordinate projection on every basis face. Agda checked all 27 declarations of the once-suspended three-cube in the generic three-slot chart, including the top cell at its prescribed composite boundaries. Further suspensions give the same example at higher cuts. [Path-lift checker](../../../tools/gray_globe_relative_paths.py) and [spatial checker and certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_matching_charts.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_relative_paths.py 5
python3 tools/gray_globe_matching_charts.py 5 5 5 --agda /tmp/GrayMatchingCharts.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayMatchingCharts.agda
~~~

The unresolved step for Q2 is to produce a single family of CaTT declarations across the adjacent-coordinate squares and to make its terms agree on faces shared with the matching charts. Neither the order test for strict paths nor the obstruction to a single arity decides existence of that family.

# Researcher Response 3

**For all pairs of globes, it suffices to compare terms on full diagonals.** More precisely, one can construct the section by induction if the following comparisons exist. The strict $k$-cell $z$ has as its top sites *every* site of degree $k$ in a rectangle, and has the full triangle clipped to that rectangle as its support. Compare a chosen representative of $z$ with a specified cut-$(k-1)$ composite on $z$, in the direction needed at that endpoint, by a $(k+1)$-term of strict identity value. The composite is given below using only earlier stages. There are no additional short-singleton lifting hypotheses. In particular, relative coherence in all degrees restricted just to these full-diagonal cells, for the generator declarations used in the induction, would imply the globe-pair alternative of Q2.

The reduction works because a single term with a formal arrow as argument transports an entire hom from a smaller rectangle. It is enough to match the two outer endpoints of that hom. Adjacent squares can consequently be treated without fixing terms for their four separate edges in a common cube substitution.

### Decomposition at the first incomplete row

Number the bidegrees of degree $d$ by their first rank. The available positions are
\[
 I_d=[\max(0,d-n),\,\min(m,d)].
\]
For an intrinsic cell look for its first row whose ranks form a proper subset of $I_d$, inspecting both signs, which have the same ranks. If there is one, write $d=k+1$. Through degree $k-1$ the tableau is that of the top atom. The degree-$k$ objects with these poles and full row $I_k$ are bit strings indexed by $I_{k+1}$. At site $(i,k-i)$ the first tag is the bit at $i+1$ when $i<m$, and the second tag is the bit at $i$ XOR $(i\bmod 2)$ when $k-i<n$. At a globe bound use the star tag. Indeed below a full row every available successor occurs, fixing the signed tags. Within the full row the shared predecessor of two neighboring sites supplies one free bit, and an exposed end below its globe bound supplies one more. Equivalently these are exactly the peak-label formula for the ideal generated by $I_k$. The Leibniz boundary shows that a site $p$ in row $k+1$ changes its own bit from zero to one. Thus the set $M$ of sites in the first incomplete row is determined by the two degree-$k$ endpoints $s,t$; the same description applies along either exterior axis.

Split $M$ into its maximal consecutive runs and execute the runs in increasing order at cut $k$. An upper site $(i,j)$ has shadow $[\max(0,k+1-j),\min(k+1,i)]$ in that row. Its whole shadow lies in one run by downward closure. There is no cut precedence between distinct runs, since such a precedence requires neighboring positions sharing a predecessor. The ordered cut formula therefore gives a factor for each run. Its intermediate degree-$k$ endpoints have exactly the earlier runs finished. These endpoints, the order, and the factors' signed restrictions are stable under globular faces above $k+1$, allowing a factor to become an identity.

For a single proper run $[a,b]\subsetneq I_{k+1}$ use the native rectangle $D^u\otimes D^v$ with
\[
 u=\begin{cases}m&b=k+1,\\ b&b<k+1,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 v=\begin{cases}n&a=0,\\ k+1-a&a>0.\end{cases}
\]
At least one bound decreases, and its set of available sites of degree $k+1$ is exactly $[a,b]$. The hom at the fixed endpoints $s,t$ is isomorphic, in every dimension, to the hom between the two iterated $k$-faces of the native top atom. Here hom includes the shift by the common lower poles. To restrict, take the native top atom's rows below $k$, keep only native sites in row $k$, and retain the whole higher part. Put a star on each native globe bound. The two restricted $k$-objects are the extreme native vertices since every available bit there changes. To expand, embed that box by the signed face specified by the frozen tags on its bounds, restore the stationary $k$-sites, and prefix the ambient lower poles. A bound $u<m$ reads its first tag at $i=u$ in row $k$; a bound $v<n$ reads its second tag at $j=v$. Those tags persist up the corresponding edge because the outside successor is absent.

These operations are inverse on positive tableaux. Above the cut this follows from the shadow bound. In the boundary from $k+1$ to $k$ the stationary labels add the same chain at both ends and cancel. At and below $k$ the two native or ambient objects have their prescribed pole difference. The same formulas respect row addition and identities. If the native box contains every ambient $k$-site, it contains the lower triangle too, and expansion is just a signed face inclusion. Call a run *padded* when there is an actual stationary $k$-site outside its native box.

### One formal arrow transports the whole hom

Take the compatible declarations on proper signed face rectangles by induction on $m+n$, with the standard globe as base. For a proper run let $x,y$ be the already typed terms for the two native $k$-faces, included in the ambient context. Temporarily adjoin a variable $p:x\to y$. Suppose there is a $(k+1)$-term
\[
             L(p):T(s)\longrightarrow T(t)
\]
whose strict top chain is just $p$ once. This test concerns one open term even when the native hom has many distinct arrows from $x$ to $y$. In the unpadded case take $L(p)=p$ and use the inherited terms at the endpoints.

Given a native $(k+1)$-cell, substitute its term for $p$. Given any higher native cell, apply the action on a maximal variable repeatedly, then substitute its term and iterated faces for the resulting formal globe. To check this action, in a head using the distinguished top variable there is exactly one active maximal slot. All top-slot multiplicities add positively and no smaller slot can contain the variable. Raise the argument there recursively, extend that leaf of the pasting tree by one globe and choose the head between the two copies of the old head on this extended tree. Its two substituted faces are literally the prescribed terms, its new top coefficient is one, and the remaining slots stay fixed. Repetition gives one globular assignment on all the input cells. The first substituted term has the native top chain since the open term contributes just $p$. Induction on height then gives precisely the native rows above $k$ and the prescribed expanded rows below them. This proves the full strict interpretation, including any padding. [Maximal-variable construction in Round 6, Response 10](../answered/canonical-gray-terms-r6-2026-11-20.md#researcher-response-10)

Construct the full-row objects and these transports in increasing order of $k$. At $k=0$ use the actual vertices. A full $k$-object in positive degree has a top-cut serialization into single-site steps, obtained by a linear extension of the cut precedence path. Except for the top atom, a full positive row has at least two sites when $m,n>0$. Its single-site steps therefore belong to proper runs at the preceding stage. Composing their terms gives a representative with the two prescribed lower poles. For an object in the iterated face closure of an already declared generator, use its prescribed term. For a cell with several runs compose the transported factor terms at cut $k$, padding to the common height by identities and using fixed bracketing. Above $k+1$ the two faces are exactly the corresponding composites: the run list depends only on the $k$-endpoints, and each padded factor has the required lower faces. In degree $k+1$ the two outer endpoints are $T(s),T(t)$. In the full-run case the next row is full, so continue at $k+1$. At the top there is just the atom; declare it at its two constructed faces. Identity towers can be assigned iterated identity terms.

For clarity about generator pins, a signed face of an atom at any height has the complete diagonal of that atom's rectangle at every occupied degree. At its first incomplete ambient row it can have only one run. Its bounding rectangle there must contain every preceding $k$-site, so it is in the unpadded case, with the generator in that signed rectangle. The lower $k$-endpoints of this case are themselves prescribed iterated faces of the native atom. Thus padding and multiple-run choices never impose a new writing on a previously declared generator face. Face data can be maintained compatibly in the induction: an iterated $j$-face of an atom in a larger box is also the same signed $j$-face of the atom of its minimal projection box, or the atom itself there. Indeed clipping the two projection ranks to $j$ keeps every occupied row; at a newly clipped bound its fixed tag gives the signed inclusion back. Use the chosen term in this minimal box whenever prescribing shared face data. This requires agreement on generator faces, with no requirement to identify arbitrary composite choices between different inherited sections.

### Building the open term from two closed comparisons

Consider a padded run at cut $k$. The preceding construction has already supplied the open terms at cut $k-1$. Necessarily $k>0$. The shadow of the run on the full $k$-row is
\[
                     J=[\max(0,a-1),\,\min(k,b)] .
\]
It is a proper interval of $I_k$ because a stationary $k$-site lies outside it. Group the $k$-sites in $J$ into one active block and keep the remaining sites as singletons. Use any topological order of these blocks for the cut $k-1$. The only interblock precedences are at a frozen join of the path, so the very same order applies to $s$ and $t$. The signed intermediate $(k-1)$-states outside the block agree in the two decompositions: the two chains of the block differ by the boundary of its upper cell and hence have equal differential.

Here is the input for the previous-stage transport on the active block. Expand the native top atom of $(u,v)$ by the run formula and take the factor on $J$ in the ordered decomposition just described. Restrict that factor at cut $k-1$. Its native value is **the signed $(u,v)$ atom itself** in the box for $J$. In detail, write this new box as $(u_1,v_1)$. Its left bound is $u$ unless the end of $J$ is $k$; in that event $u_1=m$ and a strictly smaller $u$ must be $k$. Likewise $v_1=v$ unless the start of $J$ is zero, and a strictly smaller $v$ must be $k$. Thus enlarging $(u,v)$ to $(u_1,v_1)$ adds no rank below $k$. At degree $k$ the active block retains exactly the native sites. Above $k$ it retains all the native rows. A bound that grows uses its frozen sign at degree $k$. This identifies every row in the restriction and also identifies the literal $(k-1)$-poles needed to use the earlier open term, by the generator-face convention above.

Use that open $k$-term on the two native $k$-faces $x,y$, and use its one-level raised action on the formal $(k+1)$-arrow $p:x\to y$. At the stationary positions use the single-site $k$-terms at their actual intermediate $(k-1)$-states. Paste in the common block order at cut $k-1$. The result has just $p$ in its strict top chain. Write $W_s,W_t$ for its two literal $k$-faces. They have strict values $s,t$ by the cut formula and expansion, and their lower terms are the chosen outer $(k-1)$-poles. Consequently the only inputs still needed are closed comparisons
\[
              T(s)\longrightarrow W_s,\qquad
              W_t\longrightarrow T(t)
\]
with identity strict value on $s,t$ respectively. Compose these on the two sides at cut $k$, omitting a comparison for a literal match. This produces $L(p)$ and proves the reduction stated at the start of this response. The comparisons live over the $k$-skeleton, and different endpoint pairs require no equation between their witnesses. All their strict values are identities on full-row $k$-cells, whose maximal bidegrees have exactly degree $k$. This last restriction excludes cells with additional lower-degree peaks.

There are at most $2^{k+2}-k-3$ possible underlying strict $k$-cells for these comparisons up to signed face inclusion, uniformly in the two globe dimensions. In the minimal projection box $(i,j)$ one has $0\le i,j\le k$ and $h=i+j-k\ge0$. The full row has $h+1$ sites, the two outer tags are stars, and its $h$ internal bits are free. There are $k-h+1$ boxes for this $h$, giving $\sum_{h=0}^k(k-h+1)2^h$. For example, in degree five the bound is 120 strict shapes. Several pairs of syntactic writings can have the same strict shape.

### Checks

The checker reconstructed all 8,987 cells of $D^5\otimes D^5$ from their first incomplete row or full object, comparing against independent closure under strict compositions. There were 187 full objects, 2,436 unpadded single-run cells, 2,836 padded single-run cells and 3,528 multiple-run cells. It checked the native hom in both directions, both faces of every higher expansion, the absence of generator pins in the padded and multiple-run cases, and the preceding-stage signed atom and both ordered endpoint decompositions for every padded pair.

There is also a syntactic test with actual endpoint corrections. At cut two in $D^4\otimes D^5$, the eight padded two-position pairs have native boxes of width one on an axis. For each pair the checker constructs a single open 3-term by whiskering the formal arrow with the stationary 2-step and using the two required boundary comparisons when the writings differ. It substitutes and raises that same open term on the whole native hom. The resulting 32 three-terms, 64 four-terms, 36 five-terms and four six-terms were checked at their literal assigned boundaries and evaluated as full signed tableaux. Agda in safe mode accepted the open-arrow judgment and one resulting judgment in each of degrees three through six. [Run transport checker and Agda certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_run_transport.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_run_transport.py 5 5
python3 tools/gray_globe_run_transport.py 4 5 --agda /tmp/GrayRunTransportSix.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayRunTransportSix.agda
~~~

This leaves a concrete all-dimensional question for Q2: do the two displayed changes of writing exist on every full diagonal for some choice of its object terms? The reduction and the cut-two terms do not establish them at arbitrary cuts.

# Researcher Response 4

**It suffices to compare just the two end decompositions of a full row, and only when both end sites are mixed.** In degree $k$ the remaining inputs, up to signed face inclusion, are indexed by
\[
       i,j<k,\qquad h=i+j-k\ge2,\qquad z\in\{0,1\}^{h}.
\]
For each input one needs an invertible comparison between two specified $k$-terms on the same strict cell in $D^i\otimes D^j$. There is one comparison per bit string, independent of which runs meet it. For $k=4,5,6$ the counts are respectively $4,16,44$. The rule can preserve the section on **every cell** under signed face inclusions. A strict section raising dimension by two, constructed below, identifies these remaining rows with full diagonals in smaller rectangles.

### The two end decompositions

Use the full-row objects and the first incomplete row of Response 3. Work by increasing sum of the globe bounds, and inside each rectangle by increasing cut. For a full $k$-object $z$ with at least two $k$-sites, isolate either the first or the last site at cut $k-1$. Call the complementary interval of $k$-sites $H$. There are exactly two blocks, with their order forced by the sign on the common predecessor. The $H$ factor is obtained by applying the preceding stage's open $k$-term to the representative of its restriction in the native box for $H$. The singleton factor is obtained by the same rule for its one site. Here and below the unpadded open term is the identity operation on its argument. Compose the two resulting terms at cut $k-1$. This gives two writings of $z$. With just two sites the writings coincide, including the choices on the two factors. Use the variable for a full single-site top atom.

Suppose the next row changes on one padded run $[a,b]$. Its shadow $[\max(0,a-1),\min(k,b)]$ omits at least one end of the full $k$-row. Isolate such an end. The outside singleton and its temporal position are identical on the two faces. In the native box for $H$, the moving set is still $[a,b]$. Its own native rectangle is exactly the original native rectangle for $[a,b]$: cutting off the isolated end decreases a projection bound only on the side already bounded by the shadow. The signed inclusions agree, as is also seen by restricting the upper rows twice. If $[a,b]$ is the whole available row in the $H$ box, the formal arrow $p$ already goes between its two extreme objects. Otherwise use the open term for this run supplied by induction in the smaller $H$ box. Applying the preceding-stage operation for $H$ one level higher and attaching the stationary singleton gives an open term between the two writings in this end decomposition. Changing to the chosen writing at each endpoint requires at most the comparison between the two end decompositions. Multiple runs and the full-run case are handled exactly as in Response 3.

Keep the following useful form of each open term, writing temporal products in execution order and omitting empty end segments:
\[
             c^-\,F^\uparrow(p)\,c^+ .
\]
Here $F(w)$ is a term one degree lower with a single occurrence in its strict top chain of a fresh maximal variable $w$. The rest of that chain consists of the stationary sites outside the native shadow. The two closed changes of writing have identity strict value. This form can be constructed directly. If the operation on the retained block with its outside singleton is $G(v)$, apply $G^\uparrow$ separately to the two closed end segments of the smaller open term and use $(G\circ F)^\uparrow(p)$ in the middle. Attach the changes to the chosen endpoint writings. The face equations for raising a maximal slot give exactly the required ends. Raising a substituted term at its one active leaf follows the successive heads on the path to that leaf; in particular the composite frame has the stated top chain. The identity operation is a frame of this form.

All choices here can be made on signed faces. While constructing the full $k$-objects, both end writings commute with a signed face inclusion when the $k$-row stays full: the actual list of sites is the same, both factorizations have the same order, their native terms agree by the smaller-box induction, and the operations used at cut $k-1$ agree by the earlier-cut induction. Choose their comparison in the minimal projection box of the vertex and reuse it by renaming. Use the same deterministic choice of preferred end on a given list of sites; prefer a pure end when present and, for example, the smaller first rank in a tie. For the open terms the retained end can also be chosen from the site list and the shadow alone. The smaller native boxes then embed into one another. When the cut and moving set agree, the construction therefore restricts literally, including its frames and endpoint changes. The special pure and exterior choices below use the same convention.

There is one more case in checking face inclusions. If the larger rectangle first misses a position earlier than the smaller one, the occupied row there is a *full* row of the smaller rectangle. It is one interval and its native box contains every site of the preceding full row, so it is unpadded. The smaller rectangle embeds in this native box: a new missing position at an end reflects exactly the bound of the smaller factor on that side. The rule takes the inherited term there. The same argument applies when a formerly full object is now reached by an incomplete row. If the first incomplete cuts agree, splitting into runs just freezes the same earlier bits in both boxes. This proves the face assertion by the indicated two inductions. In particular the unpadded rule has the required literal endpoints, and previously declared generator faces retain their terms.

### Exterior moves and pure end sites

Write the following for the right globe; the left-globe calculation uses the other end of the rank list with its Leibniz tags. An exterior run at cut $d$ is $[0,b]$. For a padded singleton $[0,0]$ the two ambient $d$-states differ only by changing one pure $d$-variable $x$ to its opposite signed copy. It occurs once in the strict top row of the state. The two chosen terms are the same writing by signed face inclusion. Take the depth-zero action of that writing at the formal change of $x$.

For a padded exterior run $[0,b]$ with $b>0$, cut the right globe down to rank $d$ and use the **interior** run $[1,b]$ there. Its shadow on the $d$-sites is again $[0,b]$. Use its normalized frame $F$ unchanged. Indeed the pure $d$-site is inside the hole; the other strict top sites of $F(w)$ are outside the shadow, and the faces of $w$ have degree less than $d$. By support, $F$ contains no pure right $d$-variable. Take the initial end segment of this interior template in the signed $d$-face of the actual initial state and the final segment in the signed $d$-face of the actual final state. The native $d$-terms used at these ends are precisely the signed copies of those in the clipped box: their $d$-rows have the same native sites, with the exterior tag reinserted. The two substitutions agree on the frame and on the lower poles. Thus inserting the actual formal arrow between those two terms into $F^\uparrow$ gives the exterior template. This uses the face inclusion on each closed end separately. An unpadded run still uses the identity operation. A padded run cannot include both exterior positions.

Now let a full $k$-state contain a pure end site $Y$ of degree $k$, with at least three sites in total. The writing that isolates $Y$ has a depth-zero pure step and the remaining block $M_0$, in the order prescribed by the adjacent join.
