That remaining block is unpadded at cut $k-1$: the removed site is an exterior pure change, and removing only it retains the entire shadow on the $(k-1)$-row. Thus $M_0$ uses the section in the signed box cut down to pure rank $k-1$.

Consider the opposite end decomposition, isolating a singleton $S$. In its retained block, the native full $k$-object has $Y$ as its pure end, and the more distant projection bound is now strictly less than $k$. Its preferred writing therefore peels $Y$. Inside that block the word is $Y_{\rm nat}\,M$ or $M\,Y_{\rm nat}$, with $Y_{\rm nat}$ the actual depth-zero action on its source state and $M$ the term from the signed $(k-1)$-face. The enclosing transport is at cut $d=k-1$. If padded it is the exterior template just constructed. After omitting the exterior position, the remaining interval is nonempty since the retained block contains at least two sites. If unpadded, the same argument uses the identity frame. The pure step can be moved outside this transport as follows.

For a pure step at the start, distribute the action $F^\uparrow$ over $Y_{\rm nat}\,M$. The distributor with prescribed ends exists over the two-edge string in the raised slot of each successive head, by boundary lifting there; iterating along the marked branch gives it for $F$. The action on $Y_{\rm nat}$ is the depth-zero action on the substituted state $F(y(x))$, by the marked-leaf substitution rule. The incoming closed comparison $c^-(x)$ has degree $d+1$, depends on the marked pure $d$-variable $x$ at depth at most one, and has only that occurrence of a pure right $d$-variable in its support. This follows from its strict value, the identity on the state. In its support $x$ is maximal. Depth-one naturality slides the change of $x$ across this comparison. The resulting word starts with the pure action on the **chosen ambient state**, followed by the clipped interior template on $M$: its incoming segment is the signed copy at the new tag, and its outgoing segment already has that tag. If the pure step is last, distribute over $M\,Y_{\rm nat}$ and use naturality of the outgoing comparison instead. Reassociation uses the corresponding temporal string. These comparisons are invertible in the $k$-skeleton. [Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, Theorem 3.1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.11620)

There are now three factors, the pure step, $M$ in its clipped template, and the opposite singleton $S$. Move the pure step to its prescribed end. At most one adjacent swap with $S$ is needed. Since there is an intervening $k$-site, the top generator of $S$ has right rank at most $k-2$. Its two step cells are signed copies of the same cell in the right $(k-1)$-face. The only affected variable in its support is the stationary pure $(k-1)$-variable at that corner: it occurs in the $(k-1)$-row, while the top atom of $S$ has smaller right projection. By face naturality the two terms for $S$ are therefore related by this variable renaming. Depth-one naturality on that very term supplies the swap with the two prescribed pure actions. This also works when $S$ itself is pure on the other axis. The two factors remaining after $Y$ form exactly the opposite end decomposition of $M_0$ in its smaller signed box. Their common frame is the clipped template for the retained interval and the singleton is its actual term at that state. Apply the end comparison already constructed in that smaller box and whisker with $Y$. This proves the full comparison whenever an end is pure.

Consequently only minimal boxes with $i,j<k$ and at least three $k$-sites need an input. Their number in degree $k\ge2$ is
\[
        \sum_{h=2}^{k-2}(k-h-1)\,2^h
           =2^k-4k+4 .
\]
The empty sums for $k=2,3$ are zero. Each witness is for actual parallel terms in its minimal box; the inverse orientation may be obtained by invertibility. Once these witnesses are supplied the induction gives CT for every pair of globes. In particular this construction on its own has no outstanding test for a globe pair with a factor of dimension at most two: a mixed-only row in such a box has $h\le1$.

### A strict section lowering the remaining test shapes by two

Let $m,n\ge2$. In $D^m\otimes D^n$ fix the two globular rows below degree two to be those of the top atom. This fixes the two distinct directed paths around the object square. There is a strict functor from this double hom to $D^{m-1}\otimes D^{n-1}$: discard pure sites, subtract one from each rank of an interior site, and complement the second tag, keeping stars. Apply this to rows from degree two up. Each signed degree-two row contains exactly one $(1,1)$-site because the two fixed paths have opposite traversal order. This normalizes the new bottom row. In positive degrees the axis terms vanish under the projection and the tag complement accounts for the change of Leibniz sign.

Here is an explicit strict right inverse. Denote a source basis element by $A_r^x B_s^y$, with juxtaposition standing for the tensor, and suppress the star in the notation for a top tag. Use the degree-two shift
\[
\begin{split}
 A_r^x B_s^y \longmapsto {}&
 A_{r+1}^x B_{s+1}^{\bar y}\\
 &{}+ [s=0,\ r+2\le m]\ A_{r+2}^{x} B_0^{x\mathbin{\rm XOR}(r\bmod2)}\\
 &{}+ [r=0,\ s+2\le n]\ A_0^{y} B_{s+2}^{y}.
\end{split}
\]
Use a star at a target globe bound. A tag used as a vertex bit in a correction is always an actual bit, since that correction requires one more available rank.

The interior differential agrees away from the axes by the complement. On $s=0$, the extra axial boundary is $(-1)^{r+1}A_{r+1}^{x}(B_0^+-B_0^-)$. Add the differential of the second summand. When $r$ is odd these are the two successive edges from $(0,0)$ to $(1,1)$ in the square of tags $(x,B_0)$; when $r$ is even they go from $(0,1)$ to $(1,0)$. The two endpoints are exactly the difference of the corrections on the positive and negative $A_{r-1}$ faces. At the upper bound identification of the two first tags gives the same formula with the correction absent. On $r=0$ the extra axial boundary is $(A_0^+-A_0^-)B_{s+1}^{\bar y}$. Adding the third summand's differential gives the two edges from $(0,0)$ to $(1,1)$ in the square of tags $(A_0,B_{s+1})$, including its bound identification. A correction on a facet of an interior cell cancels with the opposite facet's correction since it ignores the deleted factor's tag. For a vertex the two calculations together give exactly the fixed positive path minus the fixed negative path. Thus the formula commutes with the differential, is positive, and prefixing the fixed rows defines a strict functor by row addition. Projection recovers the input.

The section takes the smaller top atom to the larger top atom. More generally, if an ambient cell has a full $k$-row for $k>\max(m,n)$, projection followed by the section returns the **entire cell**. Above and at $k$ there are only interior sites. Below $k$ all available successors are present, so the rows are those of the larger top atom. On those rows the two correction terms supply exactly its missing pure sites, with the signs in the displayed formula. Hence the mixed-only comparison shapes just isolated are strictly the shifts of full $(k-2)$-rows in $D^{i-1}\otimes D^{j-1}$, including all their lower padding.

The checker tested 14,428 choices of end chart on padded cells in $D^6\otimes D^6$, including 208 exterior endpoint pairs, and both three-factor orders at 304 full vertices with a pure end. Agda checked the incoming and outgoing slides in degree five for a change of brackets and an arbitrary native four-arrow. The double-shift checker compared the differential on every generator and both faces of every section value. In $D^5\otimes D^5$ the double hom had 8,625 cells, every one of the 1,681 smaller cells had the stated section value, and the recovery test held on all 197 cells above the first full mixed-only row, including that row's objects. [End-chart checker and Agda certificate](../../../tools/gray_globe_end_charts.py) and [double-shift checker](../../../tools/gray_globe_double_shift.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_end_charts.py 6 6 --agda /tmp/GrayEndCharts.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayEndCharts.agda
python3 tools/gray_globe_double_shift.py 5 5
~~~

The open issue is syntactic. A smaller comparison would solve its shifted test if its transport had the two specified end-decomposition terms as faces, up to established changes of writing. The positive chain section alone supplies neither that typed transport nor a comparison at those endpoints.

# Researcher Response 5

**The double shift has explicit face maps. Each map lifts to CaTT through transverse rank two for every bound. At bound one it lifts in every transverse rank for a compatible choice of cylinder presentations. The left and right maps commute strictly on tableaux, but no choice of CaTT substitutions realizing them can commute literally, even on the generating square.** The two orders on that square admit invertible comparisons with their prescribed endpoints.

### Which inclusions the shift respects

Write $A_i^xB_j^y$ for a basis element. The section $S_{P,Q}$ of Response 4 also makes sense for $P,Q\ge1$, using $D^0$ for a point. The same formula applies when a correction summand is absent at the bound. Ordinary signed inclusions cannot make a top-atom-preserving double shift natural. For example, embed the two copies of the lower $D^1\otimes D^1$ into $D^2\otimes D^1$ at the two left 1-faces. They share the vertex $(0,0)$. Its image is forced in either small box because it is the iterated source of the lower top atom. Its shifted 2-cell is consequently the 2-source of the upper $(2,2)$ atom. The ordinary upper signed inclusions send its pure left 2-site to $A_2^-B_0^0$ and $A_2^+B_0^0$, respectively. The two proposed values for the shared vertex are distinct strict cells.

Instead, grow the left bound from $P$ to $P+1$ by a map $L^a$, with $a=0,1$. Fix every column of left rank below $P$. On the last rank use
\[
\begin{aligned}
 A_P^*B_0^b &\longmapsto A_P^{b\oplus(P\bmod2)}B_0^b,\\
 A_P^*B_1^y &\longmapsto A_P^aB_1^y+A_{P+1}^*B_0^{(1-a)\oplus(P\bmod2)},\\
 A_P^*B_j^y &\longmapsto A_P^aB_j^y \qquad(j\ge2).
\end{aligned}
\]
Grow the right bound from $Q$ to $Q+1$ by $R^b$, fixing smaller right ranks and using
\[
\begin{aligned}
 A_0^xB_Q^* &\longmapsto A_0^xB_Q^x,\\
 A_1^xB_Q^* &\longmapsto A_1^xB_Q^b+A_0^{1-b}B_{Q+1}^*,\\
 A_i^xB_Q^* &\longmapsto A_i^xB_Q^b \qquad(i\ge2).
\end{aligned}
\]
Here $\oplus$ is addition modulo two. Both maps preserve augmentation and commute with the differential. For $L^a$ at transverse rank one, the signed-face column has the required corner tag on exactly one endpoint of $B_1$. At the other endpoint the differential of the extra pure column changes that tag, with coefficient $(-1)^P$ on the positive $B_0$ endpoint. At rank two the identical pure corrections on the two transverse faces cancel. Higher ranks and the boundaries in the unchanged axis then give the chain equation directly. For $R^b$ the transverse difference is $A_0^1-A_0^0$ with positive sign. The identical calculation changes the corner $1-b$. Positivity gives strict functors by applying the maps to every row.

These maps obey both coglobular identities. After a left growth the only newly introduced label of left rank $P+1$ is pure, whose value under the next left growth is independent of its sign. This proves $L^0 L^a=L^1 L^a$ with the bounds understood. The same argument gives the right identity. Also $R^b L^a=L^a R^b$. On a basis column both orders apply the indicated tag changes. An added pure column is fixed by growth on the other axis, and its coefficient and corner are independent of the sign of the transverse rank-one label. This includes $P=Q=1$, when both summands are added to the same square.

For an ordinary signed inclusion $i^a$ on the **lower** left factor,
\[
 L^a S_{P,Q}=S_{P+1,Q}i^a .
\]
On a lower column of left rank $P-1$ the shifted interior gets tag $a$, and at right rank zero its new pure summand is exactly the one in $L^a$. At left rank $P-2$ the former pure correction has left rank $P$ and corner $x\oplus((P-2)\bmod2)$, so the map restores its required tag $x$. All other columns retain their tags. At $P=1$ the same corner rule also carries the two prefixed paths to the larger paths. The right equation is $R^b S_{P,Q}=S_{P,Q+1}i^{1-b}$ for the ordinary lower right inclusion. The complement comes from the interior column of the shift. The pure right correction at corner $y$ gets tag $y$ as required. Thus the double shift is natural on every signed face for these upper maps.

### A typed lift through transverse rank two

Use presentations respecting ordinary signed inclusions, as in Response 4. Write $p$ for the grown bound, $F$ for its ordinary signed inclusion of sign $a$, and $M$ for the corresponding map above. At transverse rank zero $M$ selects the indicated pure variables. It agrees with $F$ except at one pure corner variable $x$ of dimension $p$. Let $e$ be the new pure $(p+1)$-generator there, directed from the minus to the plus copy of $x$. All variables of main rank less than $p$ keep their names and types in every transverse rank.

For a native atom $g$ of main rank $p$ and positive transverse rank, the following also describes $M(g)$ in all degrees. Take the signed atom $F(g)$. When $a=0$, append the pure change at its target $p$-pole at cut $p$. When $a=1$, prepend the pure change at its source $p$-pole. The change acts on the unique $x$ in that pole and fixes its other sites. Indeed the pure term of degree $p$ on each of the atom's two $p$-rows is at a different transverse vertex. Only the indicated endpoint gets a different tag under $M$. In degree $p+1$ each signed row contains exactly one site of transverse rank one and main rank $p$. This supplies the same extra pure $(p+1)$-generator on both rows. The higher rows agree with the signed atom. This proves the cut formula on entire tableaux.

At transverse rank one apply depth-zero naturality in $x$ to the literal changed $p$-pole. Compose that action with the signed mixed variable in the stated order. Its type is the substituted attaching type because only that one pole contains $x$, and its strict value is the cut formula. Use the binary operation of the naturality calculus here.

For transverse rank two take the source context through degree $p+1$. In this context the up-closure $X$ of $x$ consists of $x$ and the incident $(p,1)$ variables, in main/transverse order. This follows from support and the bound $p$ on main rank. Its depth is at most one. Label the two endpoint copies of the naturality context by $F,M$ for $a=0$, and by $M,F$ for $a=1$. Label the change of $x$ by $e$. For each marked rank-one mixed variable its change type now has literally the same composite on both sides: one side uses the pure action on the affected endpoint, and the other uses the rank-one value just defined. Use its identity. Unmarked variables have the same label on both copies.

Let $u,v$ be the declared $(p+1)$-poles of the native rank-two variable. Their $p$-poles agree literally by typing. On each of $u,v$ the depth-one naturality term for $X$ now compares its $M$-substitution with its $F$-substitution preceded or followed by the common pure action. The other end is unmarked, so the naturality type has exactly this one boundary operation. These $(p+2)$-comparisons have identity strict image and are invertible: their potentially top-dimensional mixed labels were instantiated by identities, and the other labels have degree at most $p+1$. Whisker the signed $(p+2)$-variable by the common pure action, taking at its two faces the binary operations just used. These heads exist over the wedge of the pure-action globe and the mixed globe at cut $p$, in the indicated order. Put the two comparisons at its ends in the required directions. The result has literal faces $u[M],v[M]$. Its strict image is the cut formula. This construction is uniform in $p$, including arbitrary raw attaching terms compatible with the signed face presentation. [Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

**Growing a bound of one.** Here a different induction realizes the same face map in every transverse rank. Start with the rank-one strip map on the square. In the target use the subcontext formed by the two ordinary signed copies of that strip and the two new pure 2-variables at transverse vertices. These contain every label used in the substitution. To grow the transverse bound from $j$ to $j+1$, $j\ge1$, mark all variables of transverse rank $j$ in the source and this target subcontext. The sets are up-closed. The only marked ranks are $(0,j),(1,j)$ in main/transverse order. The extra pure 2-variables and their boundaries are unmarked. The inverse image of the target marks under the substitution is exactly the source marks. On marked variables the image contains its signed mixed or pure label of transverse rank $j$. On the other variables support has smaller transverse projection. Both contexts and the substitution therefore have depth at most one with respect to these marks.

Use the marked-column cylinder presentations for transverse growth. For growth in the first tensor slot these identify the two copies of a marked label with its two old signed faces and its change with the new variable; for growth in the second slot transport the construction through the odd-dimensional opposite and factor reversal. This is precisely the rank-zero and rank-one case of the signed column calculation. The unmarked pure variables are carried along as parameters. Apply naturality to the **substitution**. Lemma 3.3 gives literal agreement with the old substitution on both signed copies. For a changed variable the top chain has its single new signed label: there is exactly one top label of its marked bidegree in the old image. The extra pure label when $j=1$ is unmarked. The coefficient of the new label is one by the difference between its two old signed faces. The resulting faces and this top row determine the new tableau, which is $M$ of the new atom by the chain formula. This proves the induction. The argument makes a choice of cylinder declarations in both signed strips; it asserts existence with those choices. [Round 6, Response 10, including the signed column calculation](../answered/canonical-gray-terms-r6-2026-11-20.md#researcher-response-10)

### Why a strict square of substitutions is impossible

Start with the generating 2-variable of $D^1\otimes D^1$ and grow both bounds once. Its final strict top row consists of a mixed square $z$, a pure left 2-generator $l$ and a pure right 2-generator $r$, each with coefficient one. In **any** syntactic realization that first grows the left bound, the first image uses the intermediate mixed variable exactly once in its degree-two slots. After right substitution that occurrence is a subterm with strict top chain $z+r$. In the opposite order there is a subterm with top chain $z+l$. A single raw 2-term with the stated final top row cannot have both. Its tree of degree-two arguments counts top labels by addition: a composition head counts each degree-two slot once and a higher coherence on a lower arity contributes none. Two subtrees containing its unique $z$ must therefore be nested. Neither of these chains is contained in the other. This rules out literal commutation for every choice of representatives of the two functors.

For the rank-one terms constructed above the two composites agree on the boundary of the generating square. Their top terms both serialize the three changes $l,z,r$. When the orders agree a rebracketing compares them. Otherwise the two pure steps are adjacent on independent edges of the same path. Compare their two orders over the horizontal wedge of two 2-globes, whisker with $z$, and rebracket to the specified ends. This gives both directed 3-terms over the strict identity for all four sign pairs. It explains why the failure of literal commutation alone does not refute CT.

The checker verified the differential, the two coglobular identities, interchange, the shift equations and the cut formula on generator tableaux through bounds seven. At bound four it also checked 108 actual strip labels. Agda checked both directed comparisons on all four signed squares and the four transverse-rank-two substitutions at bound one, at their prescribed types. [Face-map checker and Agda certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_shift_faces.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_shift_faces.py 7 --strip 4 --agda /tmp/GrayShiftFaces.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayShiftFaces.agda
~~~

For larger grown bounds the new obligations start at transverse rank three. Even a lift for each face map separately would still need compatible comparisons where the two axes meet, and agreement with the end decompositions of Response 4. The displayed square supplies the first of those comparisons. The all-dimensional assignment requested in Q2 remains open.

# Researcher Response 6

**For the globe-pair alternative of Q2, four open comparisons per mixed rectangle and degree suffice.** Each compares two specified terms containing three degree-$k$ variables, each once in the strict top chain. The first and last are actual generators. The middle is a formal arrow between two specified $(k-1)$-terms. Substitution in this one arrow covers *all* choices of the internal notch bits. In degree $k\ge4$ this gives $2(k-2)(k-3)$ sufficient open comparisons, compared with the $2^k-4k+4$ closed inputs of Response 4. The open hypothesis asks for a witness valid under substitution.

### The common middle input

Write the minimal mixed rectangle as $(m,n)$, with $k>\max(m,n)$ and $h=m+n-k\ge2$. Put $a=k-n$ and $b=k-m$. The full $k$-row has first ranks $a,\ldots,m$. Denote its $h$ notch bits in increasing position order by $z_1,\ldots,z_h$ as in Response 1. The two end generators $l,r$ have ranks $(a,n),(m,b)$. Form the signed rectangle
\[
    B=D^{m-1}\otimes D^{n-1},\qquad
    A_{m-1}^{*}\mapsto A_{m-1}^{z_h},\qquad
    B_{n-1}^{*}\mapsto B_{n-1}^{z_1\oplus((a+1)\bmod2)}.
\]
The two signs are obtained by reading the first tag of the last middle site and the second tag of the first middle site. Let $x^-,x^+$ be the chosen terms of the two $(k-1)$-poles of the top atom of $B$, included by this signing. They are parallel. Introduce $p:x^-\to x^+$ in the ambient $(k-1)$-skeleton, and keep the two native $k$-variables $l,r$ with their actual declarations.

Here are the two terms of the test. Start with the end writing that peels $l$ in Response 4. In its retained native block choose the writing that peels $r$; that rectangle is smaller, so the end comparison there is already available. In the inner remaining block insert $p$ into the preceding cut's open term. Use the native singleton terms on $r,l$, the two open operations for retained blocks, and the prescribed temporal orders at both cuts. Call the result $U(p)$. Define $V(p)$ by peeling $r$ and then $l$. When both sites of a two-site native block can be peeled, either description uses the same two factors in the same order.

To check the types, peeling $l$ retains the interval $[a+1,m]$ at cut $k-1$. Its native box by Response 3 is $(m,n-1)$. Peeling $r$ there retains $[a+1,m-1]$ with native box $(m-1,n-1)$. In reverse the boxes are $(m-1,n)$ and $(m-1,n-1)$. Restriction reinstalls the native top atom's rows below the cut and keeps precisely the indicated top sites. Thus both innermost restrictions are the full $k$-cell $w$ of $B$ with bits $z_2,\ldots,z_{h-1}$. For $h=2$ it is the top atom. Both inclusions of $B$ have exactly the two signs displayed above, including on all lower variables. Consequently both innermost operations expect literally $x^-\to x^+$ by signed face compatibility. Both outer results have the chosen ambient $(k-1)$-poles.

Every operation used to define $U,V$ is determined by $m,n,k,z_1,z_h$. In a two-block split the order is fixed by the one join between blocks. The intermediate $(k-1)$-row is obtained by adding the differential of the first block to the initial row. The full starting and ending rows are fixed; the singleton differential is fixed by its end tag. Subtracting it from the total differential therefore fixes the other block's differential as well. Apply this argument first in the ambient rectangle and then in the retained native rectangle. It fixes the two orders, all intermediate states, native signs and hence the actual earlier-cut templates. The two outer bits also determine the native singleton variables. Only the argument $T(w)$ of the inner template varies; this is the argument replaced by $p$.

In particular $U(p),V(p)$ have the same strict image. Their lower faces agree by typing and their top chains are $l+p+r$: the transports use their formal arrow once and their endpoint corrections have identity image. The support lemma ensures that the two expressions typecheck with just the three indicated top variables. A $(k+1)$-term between them has identity image and is invertible in this $k$-dimensional context.

Given such a comparison, substitute $T(w)$ for $p$. At each end it remains to go between the chosen writing of the retained native block and its writing peeling the opposite end. Use its comparison from the smaller rectangle, apply the outer open operation one level higher at its marked argument, and whisker with the stationary singleton. These have exactly the endpoints in Response 4, with inverse directions available. Composing gives its required closed comparison. Induction on the ambient bounds and then the cut now gives the reduction. For fixed $h=2,\ldots,k-2$ there are $k-h-1$ possible ordered pairs of bounds. Four choices per pair give the stated count.

### Three atoms meeting along two facets

The test can be taken in the union of the closures of $l,p,r$. The closure of $p$ consists of its new generator and the closures of the two signed $(k-1)$-rows of $B$. The end atoms meet it respectively in the closures of one generator of rank $(a,n-1)$ and one of rank $(m-1,b)$. Indeed in the left intersection the bounds force $i\le a$ and $j\le n-1$; the left atom fixes the tag at $i=a$ and the middle fixes it at $j=n-1$. One of the middle's two pole rows contains exactly this top label, so the whole intersection is that facet and its faces. The right intersection follows by the same bound and tag calculation. The two end atoms meet in their common $(a,b)$ face of dimension $k-h$, already in both shared facets.

Every site of the exterior $(k-1)$-rows has an occupied successor in the full $k$-row. Its signed label occurs on an end atom or on a boundary of the middle block. Equivalently the two exterior $(k-1)$-rows are disjoint and their difference is the differential of $l+p+r$. Taking successive boundaries includes the lower poles as well. Thus the strict cell and the terms over it have support in this union.

The resulting presentation is again strong Steiner. The differential of $p$ is its embedded positive pole minus its negative pole. Their supports are disjoint, and successive signed boundaries reproduce the lower rows of the native top atom, so the new generator is unital. For strong loop-freeness, order the ambient labels lexicographically by the first globe in its usual order (minus tags ascending in rank, top, plus tags descending), then by the second globe in its usual or reversed order according to the first rank's parity. In the two pole rows of $p$, every first rank below $m-1$ has a minus tag in the source and a plus tag in the target, since its left successor exists. At $m-1$ the fixed first tag is the same, and the second tags are in strictly increasing order for this parity convention. Hence every negative facet precedes every positive facet. Insert $p$ between those groups in the linear order. This verifies the condition also for the new differential.

The following are the temporal orders after distributing each outer operation over its two native steps, with identity-image endpoint changes suppressed. Here $M$ denotes the middle slot. The bits prescribe the precedence at the two shared facets.

| $(z_1,z_h)$ | Peel left, then right | Peel right, then left |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $(0,0)$ | $lMr$ | $lMr$ |
| $(1,1)$ | $rMl$ | $rMl$ |
| $(0,1)$ | $lrM$ | $rlM$ |
| $(1,0)$ | $Mrl$ | $Mlr$ |

Even in the first two cases, agreeing on the order of the three labels leaves a comparison of their actual terms and endpoint changes. There is also a strict obstruction to deducing the last two cases merely by substituting wider atoms into the width-two fork. In that fork one whole $(k-1)$-pole of the middle atom has exactly two top labels, the two facets shared with the end atoms. If a strict labelling sends the three bare atoms to the three bare atoms of a wider test, each shared facet can contribute at most the single top label in the corresponding target intersection. Indeed its image contributes positively to a pole of both incident atoms. Those two images must account for the whole pole of the target middle atom. That pole has $h$ top labels, which is impossible for $h>2$. This obstruction concerns preservation of the three bare atoms; terms with padding or endpoint corrections have additional possibilities.

### Changing the middle arrow in a witness

An open witness $E(p):U(p)\to V(p)$ also provides compatibility with higher native middle cells. For $q:p_0\to p_1$ use depth-one naturality on $E$ in the maximal variable $p$. Both of its faces contain $p$ once in top degree. In execution order the result compares
\[
       E(p_0)*_k V^\uparrow(q)
          \quad\longrightarrow\quad
       U^\uparrow(q)*_k E(p_1).
\]
Here the actions on the faces use the depth-zero operations of the same calculus. Both paths have $q$ once in their strict top chains. Repeat on the newly introduced highest variable: at each step it is maximal, the witness has depth one and its two faces have depth zero in that variable. The displayed construction therefore gives the boundary for the next step recursively in all dimensions, with identity strict value for each comparison by the dimension of the expanded context. Substitution supplies the actual native globes. [Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, Theorem 3.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

The checker compares both nested restrictions on all signed labels of the middle box, groups their complete operation data by the two exterior bits, and checks the three support intersections, Steiner order and precedence table. It uses independent closure under composition through bounds five. Through bounds eight it finds 1,272 closed states and 364 open tests across 91 rectangles with cut specified. Agda checked the first two higher changes of a sample open ternary reassociation, through degree six. That certificate checks the stated boundary convention; construction of $E(p)$ for the mixed rectangle terms remains open. # Researcher Response 7

**For all globe pairs, two distant exchanges per mixed rectangle and degree suffice.** They are closed comparisons between the two orders of the actual singleton steps at the left and right ends. The formal middle arrow of Response 6 drops out because the two frames around it are the two open writings one degree lower. Naturality of their comparison supplies the middle square. The two equal-order cases then close by cancellation.

### Identifying the lower terms

Use the notation of Response 6 and put $d=k-1$. The degree-$d$ row in the ambient rectangle has first ranks $a-1,\ldots,m$. Write $w_-,w_+$ for the two chosen $d$-poles of the signed middle box $B$, so $p:w_-\to w_+$. In either writing, distribute the actions on native composites until the occurrence of $p$ stands alone. With execution order from left to right, the result has the form
\[
     A_U\,P_U(p)\,B_U,\qquad A_V\,P_V(p)\,B_V .
\]
Here juxtaposition is at cut $d$, and $P_i(p)=F_i^\uparrow(p)$ for a degree-$d$ frame $F_i(w)$. The four end segments contain no $p$. To obtain these expressions, use the normalized templates of Response 4. In a raised slot the comparison distributing over two consecutive arrows is obtained over the two-arrow string in that slot. Whisker it through the heads on the way to the root. Reassociate the resulting word and put each closed endpoint correction on the side where it occurred. This gives invertible comparisons with the original terms, including their literal outer endpoints. Raising along two nested marked branches is the action along their composite branch.

Consider first the writing peeling $l$ and then $r$. The upper retained interval $[a+1,m]$ has shadow $[a,m]$ at degree $d$. Its only stationary $d$-site is $a-1$. In the construction of its normalized template the end to peel is therefore forced. Its frame takes a native $d$-term, applies the cut-$(d-1)$ transport for the block $[a,m]$, and composes with the singleton at $a-1$ in their prescribed order. The retained native box is $(m,n-1)$ in both constructions. Indeed the upper interval and its lower shadow give exactly those bounds in the run formula. Within this box the second upper peel retains $[a+1,m-1]$, whose lower shadow is $[a,m-1]$. This time the only stationary $d$-site is $m$. The second frame peels it and uses the native box $(m-1,n-1)$. In the other writing the lower ends are peeled in the order $m,a-1$, through $(m-1,n)$ and $(m-1,n-1)$.

These identifications include the intermediate states and the actual operations. Splitting at cut $d-1$ separates the *whole retained upper factor* from its stationary singleton. Replacing that upper factor by any of its $d$-states leaves the singleton, block order and cut-$(d-1)$ endpoints unchanged, since these depend only on the two $(d-1)$-poles and the singleton differential. Restricting the active factor retains precisely the higher rows of the signed native box and its full $d$-row. The same argument applies inside that box. Thus the two frames use the singleton terms and the cut-$(d-1)$ open operations of the two lower end writings, including any corrections already inside those operations.

At the middle step the left end event has executed exactly when $z_1=0$, and the right end event exactly when $z_h=1$. This holds in both orders in the table of Response 6. Consequently the exterior notch bits of the lower test are $(1-z_1,z_h)$. The $h-1$ interior positions are all zero on entry to $p$ and all one on exit. In particular the two lower tests at these corners have the **same open terms**. Their signed middle inclusion has first tag $z_h$ and second tag $(1-z_1)\mathbin{\mathrm{XOR}}(a\bmod 2)$, the latter equal to $z_1\mathbin{\mathrm{XOR}}((a+1)\bmod 2)$. Hence their hole expects the two $(d-1)$-poles of the very same signed $B$. Both $w_-$ and $w_+$ are legal substitutions. With consistent bracketing the two lower open writings are literally $F_U(w),F_V(w)$.

### The boundary of the degree induction

When $d>\max(m,n)$ the open comparison
\[
                  E(w):F_U(w)\longrightarrow F_V(w)
\]
is the preceding degree's mixed comparison. When $d=\max(m,n)$ a lower end is pure. Here the pure-end argument of Response 4 gives the same comparison with $w$ free. The following spells out where the parameter enters that argument.

Call the pure end $Y$ and the other end $S$. In the writing peeling $Y$ first, its complementary block is unpadded. The rest of the writing already peels $S$ in the clipped signed box, using $w$ in its remaining block. In the writing peeling $S$ first, the native retained word peels $Y$ next. Within this native word the block complementary to $Y$ is again unpadded. Thus its two arguments are the native pure action and $w$, in the order forced by their join. The enclosing exterior template uses the frame of its clipped interior run and the signed copies of the endpoint corrections, as in Response 4. Distribute its action over these two arguments. The slide past the incident closed endpoint correction uses depth-one naturality on that correction in the pure variable. The other argument, $w$, is simply whiskered through the slide at its indicated signed type. After the slide the frame on $w$ and its two end corrections are precisely the clipped template on $w$. This follows from the definition of the exterior template, which takes the initial and final segments separately on their signed faces. If the surrounding template is unpadded, its frame is the identity and there are no such corrections.

Move $Y$ past $S$ if required. The depth-one square on the singleton term for $S$ uses only the stationary pure variable at the relevant corner. It has no $w$ in its context. What remains in either writing is exactly the decomposition peeling $S$ in the signed box clipped at $Y$, with the same argument $w$. Both orders use the same join for $S$ and the surviving block. This also covers two pure ends, for which both complementary blocks are unpadded. All these slides and reassociations are reversible above the lower skeleton. This proves the open pure-end case needed at the first mixed degree.

### Using the lower closed comparison at both middle corners

Set $e_-=E(w_-)$ and $e_+=E(w_+)$. They are independent of $p$ and have identity strict image. Naturality of $E$ in its maximal degree-$d$ variable supplies an invertible $(k+1)$-comparison between
\[
             e_-\,P_V(p) \quad\text{and}\quad P_U(p)\,e_+ .
\]
The witness has depth one in $w$ and its faces have depth zero. These face actions are the marked-leaf actions used for the frames. This is the literal boundary calculation of Response 6, applied one degree earlier. [Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, Theorem 3.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

At either middle corner $z$, write $D_U,D_V$ for its original lower end writings and $i_U,i_V$ for the changes to their twice-peeled versions $F_U(T(w)),F_V(T(w))$. Here $w$ is the native strict $d$-state at this corner. Each $i$ changes the retained native writing to its opposite end writing and applies the outer operation to that change. Choose the lower closed comparison from $D_U$ to $D_V$ to be
\[
                         i_U\,E(T(w))\,i_V^{-1}.
\]
This is the construction of the closed comparison in Response 6. Make it at every lower state with these two end writings, using the open pure-end proof at the boundary of the induction. Make the choice in the minimal box and use its signed copies. The preferred $T(z)$ is one of $D_U,D_V$. Write $c_U,c_V$ for the changes from it to those writings, taking the empty change at the preferred end, and $K_U(z)=c_U i_U$, $K_V(z)=c_V i_V$. Use inverse changes for terminal corrections. By associativity, cancellation and units,
\[
                  K_U(z)\,E(T(w))\simeq K_V(z).
\]
These are actual higher terms by the inverse laws for invertible CaTT cells and temporal reassociations. The same choice works at *both* middle corners because the native pole calculation gives $T(w)=w_-$ at its source $s$ and $T(w)=w_+$ at its target $t$, for any of the four upper tests.

Consequently the two terms between the preferred corners
\[
        K_i(s)\,P_i(p)\,K_i(t)^{-1}\qquad(i=U,V)
\]
are invertibly comparable. Insert the displayed corner changes on the two sides of the middle naturality square and cancel. The resulting comparison is open in $p$.

### Recovering the actual singleton steps

It remains to verify that using these preferred middle corners gives the singleton terms of the section on the rest of each word. Consider the left-first writing. Outside the retained block its $l$ is already the ambient singleton step. Inside, the $r$-step in $(m,n-1)$ is carried through the outer operation for $[a+1,m]$. At the instant of this inner $r$-step, consider the rule for the *ambient singleton* $[m,m]$ at cut $d$. Its shadow occupies $m-1,m$. It omits the left end $a-1$ and contains the right end $m$. Thus the omitted end to peel is forced, and it is the same lower singleton $a-1$ as for $[a+1,m]$. Its retained lower block is again $[a,m]$ in $(m,n-1)$. Both steps keep the left notch frozen throughout. Their lower frames $G$ agree because the stationary factor, block order, native signs and cut-$(d-1)$ endpoints agree. Restricting the ambient singleton after this peel gives precisely the inner native $r$-step. This follows on the higher row by retaining its unique label and on the $d$-rows by removing the same stationary site. Thus the earlier-cut operations and the native term selected by the construction agree syntactically. For the right-first writing the transported inner $l$ has shadow $a-1,a$, which forces the omitted right end $m$. Its retained lower block is $[a-1,m-1]$ through $(m-1,n)$. The identical restriction argument applies.

More explicitly, at either occurrence let $j$ be this omitted end, let $s',t'$ be the actual corners of the singleton, and let $c_j(v)$ go from $T(v)$ to the writing peeling $j$. If $S_{\rm nat}$ is the singleton in the smaller box, the ambient singleton is comparable, with its exact two faces, to
\[
                 c_j(s')\,G^\uparrow(S_{\rm nat})\,c_j(t')^{-1}.
\]
Indeed this is the defining padded-run construction before normalization. To recover the normalized version, distribute $G^\uparrow$ over the two native corrections and their middle, then compose the marked branches. The upper singleton and retained interval here are interior runs because $0<a\le m<k$. All the corrections just described are the ordinary signed endpoint choices. Any exterior specialization appearing inside $G$ is shared by both terms.

Distribute the outer action over its native two-step word and insert $c_j(v)^{-1}c_j(v)$ at the seam. The transported singleton now has exactly the preceding ambient form. On the middle step the remaining correction at each corner is $c_j$ followed by the transported inner correction, precisely $K_i$ there. Reassociation and cancellation therefore express each original writing as its ambient singleton steps and its corrected middle step, in the temporal order of Response 6. All changes used for this normalization live over the formal test context and retain its prescribed outer terms.

| Exterior bits | End steps before the middle in $U,V$ | End steps after the middle in $U,V$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $00$ | $l$ in both | $r$ in both |
| $11$ | $r$ in both | $l$ in both |
| $01$ | $lr$, $rl$ | empty |
| $10$ | empty | $rl$, $lr$ |

For equal bits the ambient singleton steps in the two words occur at the same labelled states. Whiskering the corrected middle comparison proves the test. For $01$ supply the exchange between the two actual two-step prefixes and whisker it with the middle. For $10$ supply the exchange between the suffixes. These exchanges have top chain $l+r$, no formal variable, and lie in the ordinary $k$-skeleton. In particular they ask for comparisons on the steps just identified, with their actual endpoint corrections.

Their corners have a simple description. On the degree-$d$ cube of bits, the two extreme positions $a,m$ vary independently from zero to one. All $h-1$ positions strictly between them are zero for the prefix test and one for the suffix test. In each patch the two routes have the same source and target, and the two different intermediate vertices prescribe the terms at their internal seams. The two choices of intermediate bits, the mixed end bidegrees, and the common poles specify the two patches in every rectangle. Induction as in Responses 4 and 6 now gives the stated sufficiency. There are $(k-2)(k-3)$ such closed tests in degree $k\ge4$, by summing two over the $(k-2)(k-3)/2$ mixed rectangles.

The checker peels the higher factors, records the full lower operations, and compares them with an independent peel of the two middle poles. It also checks the transported singleton and its two lower corner operations against the standalone ambient singleton, and compares the complete labelled end steps of both words. It checks both orders, both inclusions on every native label, and both signed native pole tableaux. Through bound eight this covers 708 states with a pure end in the lower degree and 564 with two mixed lower ends. A separate Agda judgment checks the five-step pasting on an arity with independent left, middle and right changes, distinct bracketings at the middle input, and the specified outer terms. These checks validate the descent and the pasting convention. The two distant exchanges on the specified terms in arbitrary degree, and hence Q1 and Q2, remain open. [Descent checker and certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_middle_descent.py)

~~~sh
python3 tools/gray_globe_middle_descent.py 8 --agda /tmp/GrayMiddleDescent.agda
agda --safe -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayMiddleDescent.agda
~~~

# Researcher Response 8

**One of the two distant exchanges per mixed rectangle and degree suffices.** The construction can be chosen equivariantly for total opposition, which takes the prefix exchange to the suffix exchange with its actual four steps. There is a further reduction when the right generator has bidegree $(m,1)$. In that case a single open naturality square supplies the exchange with both horizontal terms prescribed. Matching its vertical sides to the section requires two comparisons involving only the $(m,1)$ generator in top degree. Both results are uniform in the dimensions.

### Choosing opposite terms on the same section

On a basis label complement each non-star tag in both factors. Write $D$ for this relabelling. Then $\partial D=-D\partial$ by the Leibniz formula. On tableaux interchange the two chains of every row as well as applying $D$ to their labels. This preserves the tableau equations and augmentation, takes each atom to the atom of its reflected label, and reverses the order in every cut composition. A full state with bits $z$ is sent to the state with bits $1-z$. Signed face inclusion commutes with this operation after complementing the signs of the inclusion.

Use the corresponding total opposite on CaTT syntax. In tree coordinates reverse the children at every node, permute the positions accordingly, transform a substitution argument by argument, and interchange the transformed endpoints in each arrow type. A coherence head goes to the head on this reflected tree at the transformed type. Its two support conditions are carried to one another's signed boundary conditions in the composition case, and full support stays full in the coherence case. The rule commutes with substitution and squares to the identity under the position identification. Raising a single maximal leaf commutes with reflection because the reflected tree extends the corresponding leaf and has the two reflected endpoint heads in reverse order. In particular this applies to the depth-zero actions at pure steps. [Benjamin and Markakis, §5, on opposites of computads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.01611)

Here are the choices needed to make this a symmetry of the *same* section.

- On full rows use the same preferred end at reflected states. The bidegrees and pure ends are unchanged. When the row contains more than one site, it has a notch bit and its reflected state is distinct. Choose comparison and inverse data on one member of each orbit in the minimal projection box, and transport both directions to the other member. The signed inclusions then give the choices on larger faces. For an atom the prescribed variable reflects to the variable at the reflected label. At the top star-star atom its two chosen attaching terms are reflections of each other, so its fixed name has the transformed type.
- On a padded single run choose the omitted end by ranks as in Response 4. There is a frozen join in the minimal box. For example, when a stationary $d$-site lies to the right of the shadow of a run ending at $b$, sites $b,b+1$ occur on the full $d$-row, and the join at $b+1$ is frozen. The left case uses the join between the shadow and its left neighbour. Total opposition complements this bit and reverses the two endpoints of the open term. Thus the two keys for the template are distinct. Choose the reflected bracketing and reverse the order of the reflected endpoint segments on the partner. Unpadded templates use their signed native inclusion and the identity operation on the hole. For a run containing a pure exterior position, the clipped template construction interchanges its two signed endpoint segments under reflection. The pure singleton action is the marked-leaf action just described.
- Several disjoint runs admit either temporal order by the cut formula. At the first gap between runs, choose increasing order when its frozen bit is zero and decreasing order when it is one. Choose reversed bracketing in the second case. This gap stays fixed on all higher faces of the hom and under signed inclusion, and its bit flips under reflection. Hence a cell and its reflection use reversed lists of reflected factors, including when a factor becomes an identity on a higher face.

These choices respect the inductions on rectangles and cuts. Binary temporal heads can be taken on the two-arrow string with its two exterior endpoints. Reflection of a binary whiskering similarly exchanges the two slots and their boundary operations. In choosing closed changes from open ones as in Response 7, carry the two inverse directions as a pair, exchanging their roles on reflection. This makes the reflected composite an allowed choice for the opposite direction at the partner state. The construction requires only the literal faces of the open witnesses and the inverse laws for their closed specializations.

On a prefix patch, the two varying bits increase from $00$ to $11$ and all strictly intermediate bits are zero. Reflection reverses each path and complements its states. Its new endpoints are again $00$ and $11$, with all intermediate bits one. Thus the two paths become exactly the suffix paths, with their singleton terms transformed as above. A supplied prefix comparison and its inverse give the two needed directions on the suffix. In degree $k\ge4$ the sufficient number of closed exchanges is therefore $(k-2)(k-3)/2$.

### An open square at a mixed generator of transverse rank one

Take $d=k-1=m$, $r=(m,1)$ and $l=(a,n)$, with $m-a=h\ge2$. Index the four corners $z_{ij}$ by whether $l,r$ have respectively executed. Write $L_j$ for the actual horizontal $k$-term on $l$ at fixed $j$ and $R_i$ for the actual vertical term on $r$ at fixed $i$. The following works separately on either patch.

Collapse the left bound from $m$ to $m-1$, identifying its two signed $(m-1)$-copies and killing rank $m$. Both horizontal tableaux collapse to the same old $k$-step $q$ with top label $l$. In fact peeling the rightmost $d$-site from either horizontal step at cut $d-1$ leaves exactly the signed copy of $q$. The stationary $d$-site is pure since $m=d$. The retained block covers every $(d-1)$-site, and hence its transport at that cut is just signed inclusion. Its native $d$-row starts at $a-1$ and ends at $m-1$. The pure factor acts on the target $(d-1)$-pole of $q$ when $j=0$, following the signed copy, and on its source pole when $j=1$, preceding the copy. This is the signed-column boundary formula with the two choices of order at the last join. The two vertical tableaux are the marked-column cylinders on the two $d$-poles of $q$. [Round 6, Response 10, signed column formula](../answered/canonical-gray-terms-r6-2026-11-20.md#researcher-response-10)

Take the normalized old open template for $q$. Denote its degree-$d$ frame by $G(w)$, where $w$ is a formal $d$-variable with the $(d-1)$-poles of the native $(a,n)$ atom as attaching terms. Write $w_0,w_1$ for that atom's actual $d$-poles, so $l:w_0\to w_1$. Let $s,t$ be the $(d-1)$-source and target of $G(w)$. These are independent of $w$. For an expression in the old skeleton use superscripts $-,+$ for its two signed copies. With hats denoting the chosen pure actions, put
\[
        P_0(w)=G^-(w)*_{d-1}\widehat t,\qquad
        P_1(w)=\widehat s*_{d-1}G^+(w).
\]
These are the actual normalized *frames* of the two horizontal steps. Indeed peeling the pure $d$-site gives the binary operation above with the old native open term as its sole varying argument. Normalizing it as in Response 4 puts the old endpoint corrections outside its marked branch. Thus with the closed initial and terminal corrections denoted by $A_j,B_j$ one has, up to the specified distributors and reassociations,
\[
                      L_j = A_j\,P_j^\uparrow(l)\, B_j
\]
at the exact exterior terms of $L_j$. Juxtaposition from here on is at cut $d$.

In the old $d$-skeleton mark the variables of first rank $m-1$. The marks are up-closed by support. They have transverse ranks zero and one, so $G(w)$ has depth at most one in the marks. The attaching terms of $w$ have first rank at most $a\le m-2$, which leaves the formal variable unmarked. Label the two copies in the naturality context by the signed inclusions. Use the actual pure variables for changes at transverse rank zero and the $(m,1)$ variables for changes at rank one. The latter declarations have precisely the required naturality types because the pure end and the signed mixed end of the atom are the two forced factors on each face, with the depth-zero pure action and the native signed variable. Applying depth-one naturality to $G(w)$ now gives an open $k$-term
\[
                            J(w):P_0(w)\longrightarrow P_1(w).
\]
Its strict top chain consists of $r$ once. The old frame has the stationary $(m-1,1)$ label once, its remaining stationary labels have smaller first rank, and the pure changes have degree $d$. Equivalently the coefficient of the sole available changed top label is forced by the difference of its two signed copies in the boundary. [Benjamin–Markakis–Offord–Sarti–Vicary, §3 and Theorem 3.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

Now mark only $w$ in this new term. It is maximal, $J(w)$ has depth one in it, and its two faces have depth zero. Its naturality at $l$ compares the two paths
\[
                  J(w_0)\,P_1^\uparrow(l)
                  \quad\simeq\quad
                  P_0^\uparrow(l)\,J(w_1).
\]
This comparison is invertible in the $k$-skeleton. Inserting the actual horizontal endpoint corrections gives an exchange on $L_0,L_1$ with vertical terms
\[
           V_0=A_0\,J(w_0)\,A_1^{-1},\qquad
           V_1=B_0^{-1}\,J(w_1)\,B_1 .
\]
For instance $V_0 L_1$ reduces by cancellation to $A_0 J(w_0) P_1^\uparrow(l) B_1$. The square changes this to $A_0 P_0^\uparrow(l) J(w_1) B_1$, which compares to $L_0 V_1$. Each $V_i$ has strict value and literal endpoints equal to those of $R_i$. Thus the two sufficient residual judgments here are $V_i\simeq R_i$. Their contexts contain $r$ as the only required $k$-generator, by support. All corrections used in their definitions have identity strict image and can be taken in the $d$-skeleton. In particular these judgments involve neither a variable for $l$ nor a choice of comparison already involving both end generators. The other thin end can be treated with the right-column convention obtained by interchanging factors and taking the odd opposite.

The argument separates two uses of naturality. Applying it first to the degree-$d$ frame makes the mixed generator available as the change of a stationary label. Applying it next to the fresh maximal hole supplies the square. Directly marking the old column in the already-substituted degree-$k$ horizontal term can have depth two.

For $D^M\otimes D^3$ with $M\ge3$, every mixed test in its subrectangles now has just one top generator in each residual judgment. Indeed the inequalities $k>\max(m,n)$ and $h=m+n-k\ge2$ force $n=3$, $k=m+1$, $h=2$ and $b=1$. Thus the two specified comparisons over the edge generator $(m,1)$ for each $3\le m\le M$ suffice for this entire family. For arbitrary globe pairs, after handling both thin ends this way, the tests still involving *both* generators are indexed by $a,b\ge2$ and $a+b\le k-2$. There are $(k-4)(k-5)/2$ of these in degree $k\ge6$ and none below degree six.

The checker verified reflection and involutivity on all 8,987 intrinsic cells in the five-by-five box, including the run choices. It compared both reflected paths on 204 pairs of exchange patches through bound ten. On 72 thin-column patches it checked the collapse, the two signed whiskerings, both actual vertical strict cylinders and the common composite. A separate Agda certificate constructs a degree-five square with an independent native change and a mixed variable declared between two composite paths, using the indicated pure actions on the horizontal boundaries. It checks the pasting at literal types. The comparisons $V_i\simeq R_i$ and the general prefix exchange for thicker columns remain open, so this does not yet decide Q2. [Exchange checker and Agda certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_globe_distant_columns.py)
