                                \quad\ast\quad D^b v .               \tag{5}
\]
The symbols $a,b$ on $D$ denote the first and second *stages*. In particular $D^a v$ has the action obtained by evaluating the promotion of $H_v$ at the bridges. All the bracket adjustments in (5) preserve those evaluated actions by Response 21.

The parenthesis in (5), with exactly this first-stage action, is the reference for $h[\sigma_0]$ on $S$. The assumed elementary test on this smaller target compares it to the normal bridge
\[
                   b_*:u[\tau_0]\longrightarrow v[\tau_1].
\]
Indeed the action of $h[\sigma_0]$ is the cap correction around this reference by construction; the one-sided face replacement of Response 19 transfers the comparison back to the reference. This use of the *same head* avoids any requirement that the syntax of the first-stage promotion itself have a particular recursively assigned action.

For the second factor, first use precisely the spatial mask pulled back along the temporal substitution $\tau_1^\uparrow$ on $R\uparrow Y$. This selects an old top cap copy at $x$ exactly when $\tau_1x$ uses an $r$-slot in $S$. If there is a temporal change $H_x$ it has the same criterion: its caps contain the full pure labels, and its assigned term is local to their support. Changes of lower variables have no such slot. The ordinary substitution equation for depth zero identifies the resulting second factor literally with $D(v[\tau_1])$. The depth-one substitution equation on $H_v$, with opposition, identifies its action with the promotion of $H_{v[\tau_1]}$: the latter lower action is $H_v[\tau_1^\uparrow]$ by the ordinary temporal recursion. Thus both identifications use the actual lower terms chosen in that recursion. [Naturality and substitution, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

In (5) the second-stage labelling of the universal template also includes slots where $\tau_1x$ has smaller-dimensional support. Each additional cap change there is the identity, and the square supplied at $H_x$ is the temporal disk action. These entries satisfy the mask-extension formula above. At a changed cap copy there is just the identity comparison. At the padded pole of $H_x$, contracting an added identity acts by the unit constraint of each lower whisker, successively, by the one-whisker graft calculation of Response 22. The disk square contracts those minus-cap units onto $H_{\tau_1x}$ and expands the corresponding plus-cap units. At a surviving $H_x$ its two padded poles use only its own surviving cap copies and stationary lower whiskers, so its square retains its original labelling. The mask lemma on $H_v$ now compares the two second-stage actions. Its pole corrections are the two padded copies of the pure comparison on $v$ between the enlarged and exact changes: for one stationary lower whisker use the higher whisker on that comparison, with its unit constraint if the reduced operand is stationary. This choice agrees with the pure graft comparison, and iteration gives the assertion at every lower cut. Hence the lemma supplies the temporal action on the pure change of writing $D^b v\to D(v[\tau_1])$ at the specified actions.

Use this comparison in the second slot of (5) and the smaller-target comparison in the first. The result is $b_*[i_0]\ast D(v[\tau_1])$ with the reference action of the normal writing on $P$. The two exterior cap corrections recover the required comparison from $h[\sigma]$ to that writing. This proves the reduction in the opening paragraph. The unresolved tests are exactly the sufficient family with target of height below $k$ in Response 21, subject to the eliminations in Responses 21–23. Their top labels can still be nonidentity raw bridges between different writings even though every strict top value is an identity.

**Verification.** A three-slot substitution labels one lower input by a composite of two slots, a second by an identity supported one degree lower, and the third by a single slot. All three initial writings differ from the corresponding final ones. The checker constructs both substitutions from the same head arity, checks the boundary reduction at its literal poles, computes the exact-mask action in two ways and constructs the comparison from the enlarged-mask writing. It does this in degrees two through four, with positive and empty spatial strings. Separately, it builds actual opposed squares on a two-arrow path with only one of the three vertices moving, successively at the source, middle and target. It verifies the dependent labelling from the full mask and constructs the bracket comparison to the small-mask paste. Agda checks the pure boundary fold in degree two and the mixed comparison with just the middle vertex moving. [Checker and Agda certificate generator](../../../tools/gray_boundary_substitution.py)

~~~sh
python3 -B tools/gray_boundary_substitution.py --agda /tmp/GrayBoundarySub.agda
agda --safe --no-libraries -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayBoundarySub.agda
~~~

# Researcher Response 25

**At rank $k=r+1$, the substitution tests on all positive-height composition heads can be discharged using tests on thin heads alone. Among thin heads it suffices to test comparisons from an elementary $r$-term $e$ to its normal writing $N(e)$, under substitutions whose rank-$r$ entries are themselves normal.** Here “thin” means that the rank-$k$ head has arity of height at most $r$. The terms $e$ in this test are single head evaluations with normal rank-$r$ arguments, just as in Response 21 one degree lower. Both the arity of the comparison and the target of the substitution have height at most $r$. Two independently given, arbitrarily nested poles of a thin head no longer have to be tested against one another.

### Choosing the paths to a normal writing

Work at the inductive stage of Response 21. The temporal actions $H_t$ through rank $r$ are fixed. One can compare each $r$-term $t$ to $N(t)$ over its support, with an action at $H_t,H_{N(t)}$, by the preceding stage. The freedom used below is the choice of *which* such paths and actions to use when defining the generic rank-$k$ thin heads. For every elementary $r$-term $e$ choose the normal pure bridge $j_e:e\to N(e)$ and one such action $J_e$. If $e=N(e)$ take the identity and the disk action. Existence for the specified pure writing follows by the cap replacement of Response 18. Make the choices on the actual support with its inherited temporal selection and include them elsewhere by variables.

Define $l_t:t\to N(t)$ and its square $L_t$ recursively. A normal term takes the identity and its disk square. For $t=c[\tau]$, replace each rank-$r$ argument $\tau x$ by $N(\tau x)$, keeping every smaller argument fixed. Call this labelling $\bar\tau$. It is a substitution because an immediate pole of a rank-$r$ input has smaller rank. Put $e=c[\bar\tau]$. Promote $c$ and $H_c$ at its changing top inputs, using the already constructed $l_{\tau x},L_{\tau x}$. This gives a pure arrow from $t$ to $e$ with its square. Append $j_e,J_e$. The terminal pole is $N(e)=N(t)$ by equality of the literal type and strict value. If no input changes, just use $j_e,J_e$. The recursion descends through proper arguments and uses no generic rank-$k$ action that it is in the process of choosing. In particular $J_e$ is a chosen witness from the preceding stage.

We can calculate the same path and square using all rank-$r$ slots, including formal identity changes in stationary slots. Response 24 compares the two promotions at their specified actions. If every slot is stationary, Response 22 compares the result with the disk on $t$. If $t$ is a normal nonvariable term, either its defining head has only lower-dimensional inputs (the identity convention is included), or its rank-$r$ inputs are variables on its support. Thus this description also computes the chosen identity path on a normal $t$, up to a pure comparison with action. For $c$ on an arity of height less than $r$ there is simply no first stage. All these comparisons commute with inclusion of the support.

Use these particular $l_t,L_t$ to choose the squares $K_b$ of Response 23 on the generic thin bridges. It is helpful to spell out how to recognize the choice after substitution. For any pure bridge $p:s\to t$ on the same strict $r$-value and literal type, transport a proposed square along the pure comparison $p\ast l_t\Rightarrow l_s$ after appending $L_t$. The square agrees with the chosen bridge square precisely when the result is $L_s$. Here and below, “agrees” allows transport by the two padded copies of the pure comparison between the actual arrows. The transport is independent of the pure comparison in the bicategory of Response 21 by globular boundary lifting. Right whiskering by the padded pure equivalence $G(l_t)$ is fully faithful, which proves the criterion. It applies to any specified square on $p$, including a promotion or a substitution instance. The recognized squares compose and the disk square is recognized, by the triangle calculation of Response 23. We need a second cancellation property. If two pasted squares agree after appending the same recognized square on $d$, the original squares agree after transport at their pure faces. Indeed the appended square is invertible, the padded $G(d)$ is an equivalence, and all pure transports agree on the triangle with edge $d$. Cancel the appended 2-cell and use faithfulness of right whiskering. All these are equalities modulo witnessed comparison in a mixed context of height at most $r+1$. There is no invertibility hypothesis on $H_s,H_t$.

### Promotion preserves the chosen bridge squares

Let $c$ be a generic $r$-head and let $\tau,\nu$ be labellings into a pure target of height at most $r$ agreeing below rank $r$. Suppose that at each rank-$r$ slot there is a pure bridge $b_x:\tau x\to\nu x$ with a recognized square wherever the slot is temporally active. Then the promoted action of $H_c$ on the pure change $D_c(b_x)$ **is recognized**. This includes arbitrary supplied bridge writings and arbitrarily many simultaneous changes.

In fact $N(\tau x)=N(\nu x)$ slot by slot. The two normalizations of the outputs constructed above therefore have the *same literal terminal step* $j_e,J_e$ on $e=c[\bar\tau]=c[\bar\nu]$. Write their preceding steps with all slots present. For each $x$ the criterion gives the pure triangle
\[
                  b_x\ast l_{\nu x}\ \Longrightarrow\ l_{\tau x}
\]
with its temporal action at the pasted square and the chosen $L_{\tau x}$. First compose the promotion on the $b_x$ with that on the $l_{\nu x}$. Equation (2) of Response 23 identifies this paste with the promotion at $b_x\ast l_{\nu x}$, at its recursively pasted input squares. Its variable hypothesis is precisely that paste with the lower cap distributors. Next vary those change labels along the triangles. Use the single-step spatial template for $D_c$ with its already promoted temporal action. The arrows of this template are top variables. Response 21's simultaneous top-slot replacement changes their labels and their squares to $l_{\tau x},L_{\tau x}$, preserving both exterior evaluations. A temporally stationary slot needs only its pure triangle. Thus there is a comparison with action
\[
 D_c(b_x)\ast D_c(l_{\nu x})\ \Longrightarrow\ D_c(l_{\tau x}).
\]
Append the common $j_e,J_e$, and reassociate. This gives exactly the criterion at $c[\tau],c[\nu]$. Omitting the added identities gives the same conclusion by the unit comparisons in the construction of $L$. In particular one need not choose the action on $D_c(b_x)$ after seeing the labels: its actual promotion already satisfies the criterion.

The assertion holds for an arbitrary $r$-term $w$ on a pure arity as well. For a variable it is the input condition. For $w=c[\rho]$, the proper rank-$r$ arguments give recognized squares by induction. The lower arguments are stationary. On the slots reached by the spatial marks, depth-zero substitution identifies the composite change with the change of the substituted pure term. Depth-one spatial substitution on $H_w=H_c[\rho^\uparrow]$ identifies its promoted square with the one at those argument squares. On the temporal change of a rank-$r$ input, the pullback varies its two copies and their change exactly when the input sees the spatial marks, by the support calculation of Response 24. Use the mask comparison there if the head uses identities on further slots. The generic assertion now applies. This argument also works after labelling the original arity, by applying the substitution equations before evaluating the spatial bridges.

### Reducing the tests

Take a remaining rank-$k$ test with target $P$ of height at most $r$, as permitted by Response 24. For a source of height $k$, Responses 22–23 allow at most one arrow in each top string. Restrict the labelling to its two signed sections, obtaining $\tau,\nu$ on the common truncation. The normalized rank-$k$ inputs are pure bridges on $P$. Their actual squares are the recognized ones since each input is its generic normal writing on its support, or a disk identity. Stationary slots may be given identities. For the two signed pole writings $u,v$ on the truncation, the generic reference of the source head evaluates to
\[
                           b[\tau]\ast D_v(b_x)
\]
with the substituted square on its thin bridge $b:u\to v$ and the promoted square on the second factor. The latter is recognized by the preceding result. If thin-head substitutions are recognized, so is the first factor, hence also their product. Its comparison to the normal output bridge has the required action by the recognition criterion. Transport through the generic cap corrections by Response 19. This discharges all the height-$k$ source heads. Shallow threshold selections can use Response 13 directly as in Response 24.

Now take a thin source head $b:u\to v$ on $R$ and a labelling $\tau$ in $P$. Replace its rank-$r$ arguments by their normal writings to form $\bar\tau$. The $l_{\tau x}$ with their squares provide a spatial step between the two labellings. Its promoted changes $d_u:u[\tau]\to u[\bar\tau]$ and $d_v:v[\tau]\to v[\bar\tau]$ are recognized by the result for arbitrary terms. Apply the crossing of Response 23 to the given $K_b$ and label its spatial slots by this step. It supplies a pure comparison with action between
\[
                 d_u\ast b[\bar\tau]
                       \quad\hbox{and}\quad
                 b[\tau]\ast d_v
\]
at the two actual substitution instances of $K_b$. Whenever the square on $b[\bar\tau]$ is recognized, the left product is recognized. Compare the right product to the product of the normal bridge from $u[\tau]$ to $v[\tau]$ with $d_v$, using the pure triangle and composition of recognized squares. Right cancellation with $d_v$ shows that $K_b[\tau^\uparrow]$ is recognized. When $R$ has no rank-$r$ slots this step is empty. In particular it suffices to use labellings normal in degree $r$.

Finally $K_b$ itself was chosen by transporting $L_u$ and $L_v$. Its defining equality from Response 23 survives substitution, including the pure triangle and both padded cap compositors. Therefore it suffices that $L_u[\tau^\uparrow]$ and $L_v[\tau^\uparrow]$ be recognized on $l_u[\tau]$ and $l_v[\tau]$. Indeed their terminal writing is the common $N_R(u)[\tau]=N_R(v)[\tau]$. It need not be normal in $P$. Paste the recognized squares in $P$ to that writing, use the specialized equality, and cancel the common last bridge as above.

Induct on the construction of $L_t$ to reduce this last condition to the tests on $J_e$ in the opening paragraph. At a variable or a disk normalization it is the disk law after substitution. At a nontrivial first stage, specialize the promotion at the $l_{\rho x}$ of its proper arguments. Their specialized squares are recognized by induction, so the specialized promotion is recognized by the promotion result, using its original template with these labels. Its terminal step is precisely $J_e[\tau^\uparrow]$ with $e=c[\bar\rho]$. The subarity carrying $e$ is the full support of $t$, and restrictions to any proper argument support are variable inclusions, preserving normality of the degree-$r$ entries of $\tau$. Thus the hypothesis on elementary $e$ supplies every remaining step. Conversely, after the generic choices have been made, the square for the generic $j_e$ agrees with $J_e$ by the defining criterion with $l_e=j_e$ and the disk at $N(e)$. Their comparison survives substitution. The remaining tests consequently ask for actual instances of the original thin-head requirement.

### The remaining associativity equation

There is an explicit form of the remaining test in which every constituent comparison belongs to the preceding rank. Write $e=c[\rho]$, where $c$ is a generic $r$-head, and let $\tau$ be the second substitution. Both labellings have normal degree-$r$ entries. Write $\rho\tau$ for raw substitution of $\tau$ into $\rho$, and $\overline{\rho\tau}$ for its normalization only in degree $r$. Put $n=N(e)$. Consider the following two square pastes, with $\diamond$ denoting temporal pasting along a pure path:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 &J_e[\tau^\uparrow]\ \diamond\ J_{n[\tau]},\\
 &\operatorname{Prom}_{H_c}\bigl(J_{(\rho x)[\tau]}\bigr)_{\dim x=r}
             \ \diamond\ J_{c[\overline{\rho\tau}]} .
\end{aligned}                                                     \tag{6}
\]
Their two pure paths start at $c[\rho\tau]$ and end at $N(c[\rho\tau])$. Compare the square pastes by transport along the pure comparison between those paths. In particular the comparison in (6) concerns the two actual $(r+2)$-squares.

To check that the symbols in (6) are defined, a normal $r$-writing is a variable, an identity on a smaller term, or a single head included on its support. Evaluating it at $\tau$ therefore gives a normal term or an elementary one, even when the support is smaller than $r$. This applies both to $n$ and to every $\rho x$ in the display. The common terminal writing follows from the literal type and strict value. By construction the first paste appends precisely the chosen normalization of $n[\tau]$ to the proposed square for $j_e[\tau]$. The second paste is the chosen normalization of $e[\tau]$, written with all top slots present. Comparison of these two pasted squares is therefore necessary and sufficient by the recognition criterion, including the cases when an identity factor is omitted. Thus choices of $J_e$ satisfying (6) at every rank would give the globe-pair alternative of Q2 by Responses 11 and 21–24. The two orders of normalizing three successive labellings have the same pure endpoints; equality of their temporal squares across arbitrary arity substitutions remains open.

**Verification.** The checker constructs the common elementary expression from each of two labellings and checks literal equality, including a label with composite support and nested unit insertions. It specializes a compositor from a two-step globular graft, then varies the top changes of the single-step template along the individual pure triangles. After appending the same terminal step it checks the resulting arrow at the independently calculated recursive normalization endpoints. In a separate context two distinct formal squares admit a comparison only as supplied after right whiskering. The checker appends a reverse bridge, constructs the two disk comparisons at the exterior faces, and recovers a comparison between the two original squares with the single supplied higher generator used once. For (6) it separately constructs the two pure paths for an outer two-slot operation evaluated first on four slots, then on six slots or on five with one intervening identity. It checks that the paths differ and constructs the pure comparison with their exact endpoints. All three calculations run in ranks one through three. Agda checks the rank-one terms for the two-slot triangle, cancellation and the pure boundary of (6) in safe mode. Temporal agreement in (6) is the outstanding condition. # Researcher Response 26

**Equation (6) needs only normal outer operations. In its last substitution it suffices to check a binary split, an elementary collapse, or a labelling that uses each target top generator in exactly one input, once.** In the third case each positive input uses just that one generator. An input with zero top chain may be retained there only if its support has height strictly below $r-1$. In particular, thin inputs between different writings on a full $(r-1)$-dimensional support can be separated off as elementary collapses. These assertions concern the actual temporal comparison in (6).

### The two exceptional outer heads

Fix the preceding-stage actions of Response 25. Choose $J_e$ first for elementary terms whose generic outer head is normal on its own arity. This is a condition on the head $c$, not on the evaluated term $e=c[\rho]$. All constituents of (6) for such $c$ use only this group of choices or the disk convention. Indeed the second paste retains the outer head $c$. The other elementary terms in the equation arise by evaluating normal terms, each a variable, a disk identity or a normal generic head included on its support. A variable evaluates to a normal entry of the labelling. Thus it remains to extend the choices to the exceptional heads without imposing new equations on the first group.

There are two cases by the definition of $N$ in Response 21. A full generic head whose normal writing is a variable has arity the $r$-disk, top variable $x$, and the literal type of $x$. Write it $c(x)$. Choose a pure bridge $p:c(x)\to x$ and its square on the disk with the assigned $H_c,H_x$, using the preceding stage. For any normal $r$-label $z$, the elementary term $c(z)$ has $N(c(z))=z$. Use the substituted disk square on $p[z]$ to define $J_{c(z)}$, transporting it by pure cap comparisons to the required normal bridge. The raw term determines $z$. At any further substitution $\tau$, set $w=z[\tau]$ and $n=N(w)$. Before the pure transports, the first paste of (6) uses the path $p[w]\ast l_w$ and the second uses $D_c(l_w)\ast p[n]$. Here $w$ is normal or elementary, so its normalization square is already specified. Apply the crossing of Response 23 on the original disk to $p$ and its chosen square, with spatial label $l_w$ and its square. The change of $x$ is $l_w$ itself and its promoted square is that supplied input. The crossing gives exactly the comparison of the two pastes. Transport to the chosen normal bridges on their pure caps. Any two routes for this transport agree because the pure cap arities have globular boundary lifting. No restriction on the temporal depth of $w$ was used.

The other exception is a generic endomorphism $c:u\to u$ of strict identity value on a support of height below $r$. Its normal writing is $1_u$. If it already is this disk, every instance is normal and takes the disk square. Otherwise choose a bridge $c\to1_u$ and its square once. For $c[\rho]$ use their instances, with pure cap transports as above. Its normal writing is $1_{u[\rho]}$ and the disk action on this writing specializes literally along $\tau$. There are no rank-$r$ slots in the head arity. Both pastes of (6) therefore reduce, by the temporal unit law, to the square on the original bridge evaluated at $\rho\tau$, with the pure transports. This proves the claimed extension. In both cases the ordinary substitution recursion determines the cap actions. Neither construction changes a lower-rank action.

### Which last substitutions preserve the equation

Regard a labelling of the variables of $A$ in $B$ as an arrow $A\to B$, using pure pasting arities of height at most $r$. Normalize its rank-$r$ entries only. Such arrows form a category with composition $f\odot g=\overline{fg}$. In fact below rank $r$ both associative orders are raw substitution. A rank-$r$ entry in either order has the same literal type (determined below rank $r$) and the same strict value, since strict substitution preserves equality. Its normal writing is therefore the same. The identity labelling consists of variables. Pulling a temporal selection back is well defined under this normalization by typed support. For the threshold application each intermediate arity is itself labelled in the original box; the selection pulled back there is one allowed by Response 17.

Call a raw labelling preserving if every square recognized in Response 25 on a bridge in the source still satisfies the recognition criterion on specialization. The test includes restriction to the actual support of the bridge. For a normalized labelling this property is equivalent to (6) for the elementary terms in its domain. In one direction apply preservation to the chosen squares on $j_e$ and append the target normalizer. In the other, (6) first gives recognition of every specialized $J_e$, then of every specialized $L_t$ by Response 25's induction on terms. For a recognized square on $p:s\to t$, specialize its comparison after appending $L_t$ with $L_s$. Both specialized normalizers are recognized, including when their common terminal object is no longer normal. Compare with the pasted recognized bridge squares in the target and cancel the common last bridge. This proves the preservation criterion for the original square.

Two raw labellings $f,g$ representing the same normalized arrow are preserving simultaneously. They agree below rank $r$, and their rank-$r$ entries have the same literal types and strict values. Join each pair of these entries by a pure bridge with its recognized square. For any square on $p:s\to t$ in the source, the crossed square of Response 23 compares the two pastes $d_s\ast p[g]$ and $p[f]\ast d_t$, where $d_s,d_t$ are the promotions at the entry bridges. They carry recognized squares by Response 25. If the square on $p[g]$ is recognized, paste on the left and apply right cancellation on the right. Interchanging $f,g$ gives the converse, using recognized bridges in the other direction. If there are no changing slots this is the unit case. In particular the assertion applies to a raw composite and its normalized composite.

Preservation for raw labellings is closed under ordinary composition, by specializing a recognized square twice and using the substitution equation for its temporal labels. The preceding paragraph gives closure under $\odot$. Variable inclusions preserve by locality of the choices. Hence checking a generating family of last substitutions suffices for (6), even when several factors have to be normalized in the course of forming their composite.

### A factorization that retains lower writings

Put $t=r-1$, with $r\ge1$, and take a normalized labelling $f:A\to B$. Restrict to the support of the image of the full strict cell. This is a globular subarity by Response 21, and its inclusion accounts for any unused part of $B$. A binary split sends one rank-$r$ generator to two consecutive generators composed at cut $t$, leaving all other strings alone. An elementary collapse maps one such generator to the identity on its position and identifies its two end positions. Both are normalized substitutions with variable labels below rank $r$.

First split every rank-$r$ input whose strict image contains $m>1$ top generators. To see that this preserves the raw lower labels, take its support tree and identify its strings of $r$-generators to obtain the common $t$-truncation. On each $t$-site the full strict cell traverses a directed path. This description follows by decomposing the tree as a wedge of suspensions down to degree $t$, where the cut-$t$ composite is coordinatewise. Select an order of the $m$ events preserving each path order. After any prefix there is a section of the truncation given by the current position in each path, with all smaller branches retained. The source and target writings of the input pull back to parallel $t$-terms on the two exterior sections. For each intermediate section take the source writing with its variables included on that section. Its literal proper faces stay fixed, since the inclusions agree below $t$. For each individual step its pure support consists of the one moving edge and the stationary branches of the section. The two assigned pole writings have the signed supports there and the same literal type; choose the normal writing at these poles. Their cut-$t$ product has precisely the original type and strict value. Splitting the source slot successively realizes this labelling after $m-1$ binary splits, with equality on normalizing the top entry. Other original lower labels, including a pole shared with a different input, retain their original syntax. Repeat in the remaining slots.

After splitting, each strict top chain has size zero or one. Every target $r$-generator occurs once among the singletons, since the full strict cell of a pure support uses each top generator once. It remains to deal with zero inputs. If an input has zero top chain, its two $t$-poles have identical strict value and support. If that support has height $t$, choose one of its $t$-sites. Insert a new $r$-edge into the target string at the position occupied by this site during the zero step. Do this for every such zero input. When there are several at one position, order them by their source string order. Here is why this prescription is consistent.

Collapse the target's $r$-strings to their $t$-positions. All positions of a given source $t$-site then have the same strictly labelled $t$-cell, because each intervening $r$-cell projects to an identity. Any surviving target $t$-site belongs to at most one of these source supports. Indeed choose one position per source site and form the strict $t$-section. Its image in the truncated target uses each $t$-generator at most once. If two chosen sites contributed the same target site its coefficient would be at least two. On the unique source string that reaches a given site, the target position is nondecreasing, and every pre-existing single event there advances it by one. These facts also hold when the original target has height exactly $t$, with zero pre-existing edges. No insertion is needed for a support of height less than $t$.

On the enlarged target, include each original $t$-pole writing on its indicated section, placing it before or after each inserted edge according to the source position. More explicitly, each occurrence of a target $t$-variable is replaced by the variable at the corresponding position of the enlarged string. Its lower faces are identical. Variables of smaller dimension and their raw expressions are left fixed. A pre-existing positive step advances its original edge and no inserted edge. Each promoted zero step advances exactly its own inserted edge. All other coordinates are fixed at that step. The normal writing between its assigned raw poles exists by the same one-edge support argument as for a split. At a zero input with smaller-dimensional support, both pole writings and the old normal bridge remain available verbatim. This constructs a normalized labelling whose positive inputs use the target top generators bijectively, one apiece, and whose remaining inputs have support of height below $t$.

Collapsing the inserted edges one at a time gives the original labelling after top normalization. On the $t$-poles this equality is literal: the variable replacements just described project to the original variables, including repeated occurrences inside a compound writing. On a promoted input the projection may be a composite weak bridge between different writings. Its strict value and actual type determine exactly the original normal bridge. Thus the factorization has binary splits first, then the specified singleton labelling, then elementary collapses and a variable inclusion. Restricting a singleton labelling to a term support retains its singleton and smaller-support properties on the resulting image support. A restriction of a split or collapse affects its one edge if present and is a variable inclusion otherwise. This justifies using the factorization also in the restriction clauses of the preservation test.

For the remaining globe-pair criterion one may consequently take the two other levels of (6) to be a normal head and a labelling normal in degree $r$, and take the last level from this list. In the singleton case the change of lower writing around its one positive generator can still be nontrivial. The proof of the factorization gives actual substitutions, while temporal preservation of these generating labellings in every rank remains to be proved.

**Verification.** The checker factors a map of three top strings. One input traverses two sites, other inputs have zero top chain between different lower writings, and another entire string is supported one dimension lower. It verifies every binary split and every single-edge collapse under normalized composition, the bijection of positive generators, and literal equality of every resulting lower entry. It repeats the calculation with the independent events in the opposite order and in ranks two through four. In a separate test it builds the two temporal pastes for the exceptional unary head with three moving top inputs. It specializes the same disk square at a nested composite and at its normal writing, transports along the comparison of the two pure paths, and checks a higher term with the resulting two pastes as its literal faces. This test uses top-degree temporal marks, where the temporal context is itself globular; the arbitrary-depth argument uses the crossing proved in Response 23. Agda checked the rank-two factor substitutions and a top comparison, and the rank-one temporal judgment, in safe mode. ### Comparing pastes in a strip with extra pure cells

We work inside the $s$-fold hom. Its raw operations are realized by $s$ suspensions of CaTT operations followed by substitution. Both of these padded terms lie entirely on the side and are parallel. The side property supplies the next invertible change. Every original generator is stationary. This defines a labelling on the marked cylinder through degree three. All expressions involved are finite even if the auxiliary vocabulary is infinite.

The ordinary change on an arbitrary term through degree three now exists. The lowest possible mark is an arrow, so generic head actions through depth two and their substitution recursion apply. ### Realizing a side through successive lower whiskers

Write $W$ for whiskering on one fixed side by a single $(j+1)$-cell at cut $j<s$. We construct a side for any list of the successive occupied cuts of Response 21. Initially use the path itself. It has the stated lifting property by globular boundary lifting. Its objects and letters are realized by the corresponding cells of the suspended pure path. There is a realization of $L$ extending that of $V$ by the actual rail. This remains well typed when the common formal pole already realizes to a composite. Flattening the $V$-part gives a retraction with section onto $L_0=\Sigma^s[m]\vee_j D^{j+1}$, a globular pasting arity, allowing either order of gluing. Write $q_L$ for this retraction.

For parallel terms of dimensions $s+1,s+2,s+3$ in $L$ whose projections agree strictly in $L_0$, there is an invertible comparison, provided the necessary boundary changes are equivalences and the comparison of the projections is invertible. In the application both conditions hold. Here are the details. Mark the suspended extra generators of $V$ and keep the new rail fixed. On its generators the change from the identity to the section of $q_L$ followed by $q_L$ (first project, then include) is the suspension of the change just constructed on the side. The change faces suspend literally, since the $2s$ additional poles are stationary and the remaining cut indices increase by $s$. These generator changes give the labels for changing arbitrary raw expressions of $L$. A term through rank $s+3$ encounters its first mark in rank at least $s+1$, so Response 13 applies even to a head involving the new rail. The resulting changes are equivalences by the preceding top-letter argument. The projected comparison is invertible because $L_0$ has height at most $s+1$. Use the same cancellation to transfer it. Every term above $s+1$ in $L$ is invertible if the generators of $V$ above its arrows are invertible. Suspension takes the inverse data to inverse data in the suspended cuts, and the extra rail has dimension at most $s$. [Depth-zero and depth-one changes under substitution, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

Construct the next side $V'$ as follows. Its vertices realize in $L$ to $W(i)$ and its distinguished arrows to $W(a_i)$. Give a name to each raw arrow of ambient degree $s+1$ between these realized vertices whose projection has the strict value of the whiskered interval. In higher degrees give a name to every raw cell in $L$ at the realization of specified parallel formal faces. Index a name by the raw expression in $L$ and by its formal faces. Use the distinguished name for $W(a_i)$ at its vertex faces. This is an inductive definition by degree. It gives a substitution from $\Sigma^s V'$ into $L$. The projection of $V'$ to the weak path is defined on arrows by their intervals and on higher names by globular boundary lifting on the path. For two arrow expressions from $i$ to $j$, their realizations project to the same strict whiskered interval by strict composition. In higher degrees equality of the projected strict values follows from parallelism and the height of $L_0$. The comparison in $L$ just proved can therefore be named at the *specified* formal faces in each of the three required degrees. Each name above the arrows is itself invertible. Indeed its realization has inverse data in $L$, and the inverse and each successive unit or counit witness can be named at the corresponding formal inverse-data faces, inductively at every finite depth. Closure under operations then gives the assertion for arbitrary terms. This proves the side property at the next cut. Iterating gives the two required cap presentations. A lower occupied cut makes every vertex and edge of the temporal path active by upward closure. Thus attachment along two separate sides in that case is valid. Without such a cut the partially identified base strip can be used directly.

### Realizing both orders of normalization

We give the reindexing and change calculations because arguments based only on the strict value of a pasted square would miss the comparison in (6). Use suspended path terms for pure words, their bridges and the comparison between the two pure paths. The normal bridge between temporal words is of this form. Its support is a suspended interval, and its coherence type is the suspension of the type between the two corresponding ordinary words. The disk convention also suspends. Realize the distinguished verticals and mixed squares of the strip by the changes of the positions and letters. Their boundaries match by construction of the distinguished cap arrows. For a word, its Response 21 change is represented by the ordinary strip paste. At each binary join the two cap compositors distribute a fixed whisker over that join. At a single lower cut this distributor is a term in $L$ and has a name with exactly the required formal faces. For several cuts the compositor is the paste of the whiskered previous compositor and the new one, both represented at the next stage. The unit comparator for the empty word works by the same construction starting with the cap of an identity. The remaining associators, unitors, and whiskers by the exterior verticals are ordinary operations in the $s$-fold hom. In particular this gives a representation of the temporal disk action even when the sole surviving position moves.

For a bridge from a word to its normal path the two boundaries of its square are represented 2-pastes in this strip. On projection to the original strip their strict values are the same. The pure cap comparisons become identities, and the word change traverses the mixed squares of the interval from right to left once, with an empty block contributing none. The degree-two assertion for the enlarged strip gives a 3-term between these boundaries. Choose such a term once on each elementary word's support and realize it as $J_e$. For a word already normal use its disk action, which is represented by the unit construction. These choices depend on the syntax of the elementary expression and its inherited marks and are available at every occurrence of that expression.

A subdivision gives an actual labelling from the old strip into the new enlarged strip. Before any lower whisker it sends a letter to its block word. At each lower cut extend the already constructed labelling to $L$ by keeping the rail. For a name in the old next side take the name of its substituted raw expression with the substituted formal faces. In particular an old distinguished letter goes to the name for the whiskered *whole block*. A composite of new distinguished letters represents the product of the individually whiskered letters. The distributor joins these two expressions. Give an old mixed square the word action with exactly those compositors. Apply the same rule to an empty block with the unit comparator. This construction specializes every raw representative of a $J_e$, including its interior proof, with its displayed word-action faces. At cuts without a rail the same prescription is ordinary substitution of the block word. Thus the upper route in (6) is represented. For the product $\diamond$ the last cap whisker is by a position change at cut $s$. Its own distributivity pastes use only suspended ordinary operations, since their cuts are at least $s$.

For the promotion in the lower route we need the change of a represented 2-term when its letters move by pure bridges and the mixed squares move by the supplied $J$'s. One can choose the auxiliary depth-one spatial construction to commute with suspension on suspended heads with stationary new poles. At depth zero this holds literally by the one-step graft. At depth one the generic head needs only the previously fixed depth-zero boundary actions. On such a suspended head choose the suspension of the action on the desuspended head with its pulled-back marks. Its faces, strict value and opposed orientation are the required ones. Repeated desuspension stops uniquely at the first nonsuspended head or active new pole. Use the ordinary head construction elsewhere and extend by substitution. This change of choice also satisfies the comparison laws of Responses 23–25. In their generic one-band opposed cylinder parallel candidates agree by the strip calculation and Response 13; variation of the change labels then gives the same laws on labelled heads.

In detail, through the cap tower assign the two spatial evaluations and change of each cap name used by the represented term. Below the tower these are the pure path substitutions and their ordinary actions. At a new cut the two evaluations of a name for $z$ are the names for its two evaluations in $L$, indexed by the evaluated formal faces. For the change of a moving arrow name take the name for its evaluated depth-zero change there. For a moving 2-cell name apply depth at most one to its raw $z$ in $L$, with the changes already assigned to the preceding side and the rail stationary. Name the resulting term at the formal change declaration. This declaration has precisely its raw faces in $L$. Its lower changes are the depth-zero changes of the arrow expressions in the faces, which commute with suspension and substitution, and a whisker in the declaration varying only its top operand is literally the higher whisker. These equalities also prove the assertion inductively if an earlier cap label is composite. Use the pulled-back spatial mask at each head. Thus a stationary name has the same two evaluations and requires no change. This constructs the change of a represented cap expression with its specified faces, one lower cut at a time. For a distinguished arrow its change is the cap of the pure bridge by the same top-operand whisker calculation. At a mixed square the opposed change declaration consequently has exactly the two padded bridge faces and the two word actions of its supplied $J$. Use its already chosen strip representative. All position changes are stationary in this spatial direction. Apply the suspended ordinary spatial action to the remaining strip expression with these labels. By substitution it realizes the promotion of the originally chosen $H_c$, with the supplied squares on its actual inputs. This accounts for the second route of (6), including its terminal normalization.

Finally transport by the two cap instances of the pure comparison of paths. The comparison is a pure suspended path term, so these cap instances and the exterior whiskers are represented by the same rules. The two resulting 3-pastes have common formal faces: their word actions are the recursive pastes at the same initial and final words, and their pure path faces after transport are the same two specified path writings. In particular at each occupied cut a cap of a whole word uses the name indexed by that word at the previous cut, whether the word occurred directly or by the block substitution above. The degree-three assertion for the enlarged strip now supplies a 4-term at these faces. Suspend and realize it to obtain (6). Its strict image in the temporal cylinder is an identity by dimension. The proof supplies no such common strip for a singleton labelling that changes a lower pole writing or for a middle labelling on several branches at a lower cut. The preservation tests of Response 26 for these arities, and hence the general questions Q1–Q2, remain open.

**Verification.** The checker realizes the entire suspended ordinary two-square strip by a dependent substitution into a fully marked temporal cylinder. On each cap it constructs the composite distributor through two or three strictly lower cuts. It checks that the final rail attaches to the literal composite face produced by the earlier whiskers. It inserts a unit at the join of the last two distributor stages and compares two modifications there, one acting on the first stage only. It frames the resulting comparison by the two actual mixed squares and the opposite cap distributor. It checks that both boundary pastes contain both specified square generators once, that the modifications are distinct and parallel, and that the degree-six or degree-seven comparison has precisely those modifications as its faces. These are instances of the strip calculation. The general subdivision assertion uses the lifting and substitution arguments above. Agda checked the rank-three example in safe mode. python3 -B tools/gray_temporal_cap_panels.py --agda /tmp/GrayCapPanels.agda
agda --safe --no-libraries -i /tmp -i src /tmp/GrayCapPanels.agda

# Researcher Response 28

**In the remaining deep-threshold tests of (6), the last substitution may split or collapse any number of top edges without imposing an equation on the choices.** The middle substitution may have arbitrary branching, differently written lower poles, and inputs supported in smaller dimension. The choices of the elementary squares must be made together as described below. Consequently only the singleton labellings of Response 26 need to be tested for preservation. There is also a different sufficient family. One may test last labellings in which each source top edge advances **one edge at every target site belonging to its lower pole**. Such an edge can advance several sites at once. The lower pole writings in this family remain arbitrary.

Write $s=r-1$. Work at the induction stage of Response 25 with its preceding-stage actions, and take the lowest temporal marks in degree $d<s$. The shallower cases of the original head construction are covered by Response 13. Call a normalized labelling a *subdivision* if its entries below $r$ are variables and each top edge maps to the normal writing of its consecutive block, possibly empty. Restrict throughout to the support of the output, including a final variable inclusion when necessary. We use $fg$ for raw substitution in traversal order and $f\odot g$ for composition followed by normalization of its rank-$r$ entries.

### Calculating with a bridge and its square

Use the groupoid whose objects are writings of a fixed pure $r$-cell at a fixed literal type together with their temporal actions. An arrow consists of a pure bridge and a square on it. Identify two arrows by the pure comparison of their bridges and an action on that comparison at the given squares. The pure comparison is unique up to further comparison in the globular support. The cap transport, pasted product, disk unit and inverse from Responses 21, 23 and 25 give the groupoid laws. For example, paste a square and its reverse, contract the two padded pure inverse pairs, and cancel the square against its inverse at cut $r+1$. This gives the unit class even when the two vertical sides are noninvertible. Equalities in this groupoid give actual $(r+3)$-terms after the indicated pure-face transport. In a stationary support only the pure comparison is required.

For a pure $r$-term $t$ with a specified action write $t_*$ for promotion of changes at its top inputs and their supplied squares. It respects products by the two-stage comparison of Response 23, arrows equivalent by a comparison with action by the simultaneous replacement of Response 21, and units by Response 22. Hence it respects inverses. It specializes along substitutions, with the identity-slot corrections of Response 24. In particular, for a recursively evaluated term $E=c[\alpha]$ and changes $z$ of the target top variables,
\[
                         E_*z=c_*\bigl((\alpha_x)_*z\bigr)_x .
\]
This uses the depth-zero substitution equation on the pure output and its depth-one opposed version on the temporal action. For a square $K:t\to u$ the crossing of Response 23 gives the following other equality. Suppose two labellings $a,b$ agree below $r$, and $z$ consists of bridges with squares from their top entries in $a$ to those in $b$. Then
\[
                         K[a]\,(u_*z)=(t_*z)\,K[b].                 \tag{7}
\]
Here and below square specialization uses the temporal labelling on every argument. Identity changes may be included in all slots. These laws apply to any supplied $K$, before deciding which squares to use as normalizers. [Crossing at the two actual substitution instances](#researcher-response-23) · [Naturality and substitution, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.11620)

### Two subdivisions on an arbitrary normal head

For each normal writing $t$ and subdivision $f$ we first construct a square class $B_t(f):t[f]\to N(t[f])$. Take the unit when $t$ is a variable. For a thin normal head its support has height at most $s$ and a subdivision on this support is a variable inclusion. This also gives a unit. For a temporal path head use Response 27. For any two subdivisions $f,g$ put $h=f\odot g$ and $z_i=B_{f_i}(g)$ on each top edge. We claim
\[
                B_t(f)[g]\,B_{N(t[f])}(g)=(t_*z)\,B_t(h).          \tag{8}
\]
For a variable the sole nonunit factor is the same $z_i$ on both sides. The assertion on paths is precisely (6) of Response 27.

For a general full normal head use its signed $s$-truncation $S$ with its two pole writings $u,v$. Expand its $s$-sites by strings of lengths $A=(A_i)$. Denote by $t_A$ the normal term at those pole writings included on the two exterior sections. The deep selection on each string is constant, so one formal edge per site suffices for all lengths, including zero. Use the one-edge template $T$ of Responses 20–21 for these $S,u,v$, with its specified action. It represents $b*_s Dv$, or just $Dv$ when the writings agree. Write $w_A$ for the tuple of flat string words, with their chosen actions. There is a square class
\[
                           k_A:T[w_A]\longrightarrow t_A.
\]
For positive-height heads this is the face adjustment used to define their actions. For the all-empty expansion it is the disk contraction of the promotion, followed by the right unit on $b$ when present, from Response 22. Thus it ends at the previously assigned thin-head or disk action even in this case. Choose $k_A$ once for each expansion with its given pole writings. Sites of smaller height retain their single inclusion.

A subdivision $f:A\to B$ induces in each site the path normalization $b_A(f):w_A[f]\to w_B$ of Response 27. Work componentwise in this tuple. Set
\[
             B_{t_A}(f)=k_A[f]^{-1}\;T_* b_A(f)\; k_B .            \tag{9}
\]
The two instances of $T$ have the same lower labels because the substitutions are variable inclusions there and preserve both exterior sections. When the expansion $B$ is empty, further subdivisions on its support are inclusions, $b_B$ consists of units, and (9) for such a further map is itself the unit. When $t_A$ is a single variable the affected support is a disk with one temporal slot, so the path convention applies. The temporal template in this case is its single edge with the given slot square. Thus the separate path choices in (8) need no second choice for a branching head. As always the construction is made on minimal support.

In the left side of (8) for $t_A$, the adjacent $k_B[g]$ and its inverse cancel. The path equations (8) in the slots of $T$ replace $b_A(f)[g]\,b_B(g)$ by $(w_A)_*z\, b_A(h)$. Promotion carries this equality through $T$. Apply (7) to $k_A$ with the changes $z:fg\to h$. It moves $(T[w_A])_*z$ past $k_A[fg]^{-1}$ and replaces that factor by $k_A[h]^{-1}$, leaving $(t_A)_*z$ at the start. What follows is (9) at $h$. This proves (8) on every branching arity. Only binary composition and promotion of bridge squares were used above the path result. In particular $T$ may itself have a deeply marked, branching lower term.

### Recovering a common arity from a middle labelling

Let $\rho:Q\to P$ be any normalized middle labelling of full image support. Project each target $r$-string to its single $s$-position in the common truncation $S_P$. This is a raw substitution, sending edges to disk identities. For a source $s$-site $I$ let $u_{I,j}$ be the projected raw label of its $j$th position, in order from $j=0$. Their strict $s$-values coincide since each intervening $r$-edge projects strictly to an identity. The terms $u_{I,j}$ can differ. Let $T_I$ be the set of target $s$-sites occurring in this value.

The nonempty $T_I$ partition the target $s$-sites. Choose one position per source site to form a strict $s$-section of $Q$. In the image each target site occurs at most once by the unit coefficients of a cell in a globular tree. Every such site occurs because the full image uses the whole target and projecting either signed full section of that image onto the truncation uses each site. For a fixed $b\in T_I$ each actual position label of $I$ uses exactly one position in the string at $b$. Write its offset as $p_{b,j}$. Positions are nondecreasing along $I$ by the path order in the strict $s$-fold hom. They start at zero and end at the target string length by full support. Shorter branches need no coordinates.

Construct $\widehat P$ from $S_P$ by giving each $b\in T_I$ one edge for **every** source edge in $I$. In particular insert an edge also when $p_{b,j+1}=p_{b,j}$. Define $\alpha:Q\to\widehat P$ below $s$ by the original lower labels. At the $j$th position of $I$ include $u_{I,j}$ on the section with offset $j$ at the sites of $T_I$. The section elsewhere does not affect this term. For the next source edge take the normal $r$-writing at these two pole terms, moving once in each of the specified sites. For existence, first join $u_{I,j}$ to $u_{I,j+1}$ by a pure bridge on their common support on the first section, then promote $u_{I,j+1}$ along those individual site edges. Their product has the indicated type and strict value. When $T_I$ is empty only the pure bridge is needed. At rank $s$ the proper faces stay literal because all the sections agree below $s$.

There is a subdivision $f:\widehat P\to P$ with lengths $p_{b,j+1}-p_{b,j}$. It recovers all lower labels literally. In an $s$-term on a section, projecting and then restoring its used positions recovers the initial substitution on every occurring variable. On an $r$-edge, the two calculations now have the same literal type and move through the same interval at every site. Top normalization therefore gives
\[
                               \rho=\alpha\odot f.                   \tag{10}
\]
A further subdivision $g$ changes the offsets by the partial sums of its block lengths and leaves the projected raw labels $u_{I,j}$ identical. Hence the construction on $\rho\odot g$ gives literally the same $\widehat P,\alpha$, with subdivision $h=f\odot g$. This includes the case where every interval at an entire site goes to length zero. Computing the construction on $\alpha$ gives $\alpha$ again with all block lengths one. The temporal selection on $\widehat P$ is fixed by its truncation at degree $d<s$, including at the inserted sites, so these literal identifications also identify the temporal contexts. Restricting to the full image at the outset makes (10) independent of unused target branches.

### Extending the square choices and checking (6)

Take a normal generic $c$. On the arity of (10) put $E=c[\alpha]$ and $n=N(E)$. Choose a preceding-stage bridge square $K:E\to n$ there, once per such elementary expression and inherited selection. Use the disk if $E=n$. The syntactic head and its substitution determine $E$'s data, and the preceding paragraph makes this choice independent of the later lengths. Put $a_x=\alpha x$ for the top slots of $c$. Each $a_x$ is normal. Choose the square class for $e=c[\rho]$ to be
\[
 J_e=\bigl(c_*(B_{a_x}(f))_x\bigr)^{-1}\;
                       K[f]\; B_n(f).                               \tag{11}
\]
The inverse starts at $c[\rho]$ and ends at $E[f]$. The terminal writing is $N(n[f])=N(e)$ by equality of the literal type and strict value. Transport the class to the prescribed pure bridge $j_e$ by the cap adjustments. Formula (11) and its unit convention extend (9). Indeed for a subdivision of a normal generic head the recovered $\alpha$ is the identity on its support, $K$ is the disk, and all the $B_{a_x}(f)$ are units on the normal blocks. If $c[\rho]$ itself is normal and $c$ has full height, its arguments are the variable inclusion of that generic head on its support, so (11) gives the unit. An identity head over a smaller arity may have a compound argument and still be normal; its instances take the disk directly. For any thin head the support of $e$ has height at most $s$, so a last subdivision on that support only includes its existing square. These conventions also cover empty image strings.

To verify (6) let $g$ be the last subdivision and $z_i=B_{f_i}(g):f_i[g]\to h_i$ as in (8). Write $v_x=N(a_x[f])$, $m=N(n[f])$ and abbreviate tuples by omitting the indices. The upper route in (6) is
\[
       \bigl(c_*B_a(f)[g]\bigr)^{-1}\; K[fg]\; B_n(f)[g]\;B_m(g).
\]
Here the specialized promotion has the specialized lower arguments of $c$. By (8) the last two factors are $n_*z\;B_n(h)$. Crossing (7) for the *chosen* $K$ rewrites $K[fg]\,(n_*z)$ as $(E_*z)\,K[h]$. The latter promotion is $c_*(a_*z)$. Apply (8) a second time, now separately to every normal $a_x$:
\[
               B_a(f)[g]^{-1}\,(a_*z)=B_v(g)\,B_a(h)^{-1}.
\]
The tuple of equalities may be promoted through $c$. The resulting expression is
\[
                      (c_*B_v(g))\;(c_*B_a(h))^{-1}\;K[h]\;B_n(h).
\]
Its last three factors are exactly (11) for $c[\rho\odot g]$, by the invariance of $\alpha$ in (10). Its first is the required promotion on the actual input squares of (6), since $v_x=\rho x$ and (11) on the subdivision instances of these normal inputs is (9). This proves the equation with its specified square on both sides. Response 26 extends the choices to the exceptional outer heads. By its preservation criterion, subdivisions are therefore preserving for these choices even when a recognized square is specialized after a nonvariable lower labelling.

For the remaining test one can use the singletons of Response 26 because the other factors there are subdivisions. Alternatively, (10) factors **every last labelling** as an $\alpha$ of the simultaneous one-edge form followed by a preserving subdivision. Thus it suffices to prove preservation for those $\alpha$ with choices of $K$ satisfying their equations. The two sufficient families impose different conditions on a last map. In particular an $s$-pole of $\alpha$ may be a compound writing using several target sites. Its changes of lower writing at consecutive positions survive (10). Compatibility on these maps, which is still needed to conclude Q2, is open.

### Restricting the middle labelling as well

For the simultaneous one-edge version of the test, the middle labelling need only be a subdivision or itself of simultaneous one-edge form. This follows from a cancellation law for three successive labellings. For normal $t$ denote its chosen square by $b_t(u):t[u]\to N(t[u])$, with units and support restrictions as above, and abbreviate the normal term $N(t[u])$ by $t\odot u$. Write $E(u,v)$ for (6) on this pair of labellings for every normal head on the source and its support restrictions. Thus
\[
                b_t(u)[v]\, b_{t\odot u}(v)
                      = t_* b_u(v)\, b_t(u\odot v),
\]
where $b_u(v)$ denotes the tuple over the top entries of $u$. If $E(u,v)$ and $E(v,w)$ hold, then
\[
                      E(u,v\odot w)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
                      E(u\odot v,w).                                \tag{12}
\]
Indeed start from $b_t(u)[vw]\, b_{t\odot u}(v)[w]\, b_{(t\odot u)\odot v}(w)$. Applying $E(v,w)$ to $t\odot u$, then crossing $b_t(u)$ as in (7), gives
\[
     t_*\bigl(u_* b_v(w)\bigr)\ b_t(u)[v\odot w]\ b_{t\odot u}(v\odot w).
\]
Applying $E(u,v)$ first and specializing its comparison along $w$ gives instead
\[
      t_*\bigl(b_u(v)[w]\bigr)\ b_t(u\odot v)[w]\ b_{t\odot(u\odot v)}(w).
\]
The tests in (12) replace the last two factors in these respective expressions. After replacement the results agree by $E(v,w)$ on the individual normal entries of $u$, promoted through $t$, and associativity of normalized substitution. All the displayed prefixes are invertible in the groupoid. Cancellation proves equivalence in both directions with the given middle and end squares. The calculation is local on the support of $t$ and of each input.

For a last simultaneous one-edge map $g$, write the middle map as $\alpha\odot f$ by (10). Factor $f\odot g=\beta\odot h$ by (10) again, with $\beta$ simultaneous one-edge and $h$ a subdivision. If every pair of simultaneous one-edge maps passes, apply (12) to $\alpha,\beta,h$. The two equations ending in $h$ already hold, so this gives $E(\alpha, f\odot g)$. If the pairs consisting of a subdivision followed by a simultaneous one-edge map also pass, apply (12) to $\alpha,f,g$ to obtain $E(\alpha\odot f,g)$ as desired. Restrictions of the simultaneous one-edge maps used here still have that form on the image support: restricting a source string selects an interval of its positions, which selects exactly the same interval at each of its owned sites. A stationary source position alone selects just its section. Thus (12) uses the same two families on its smaller supports. These remaining equations retain nonvariable lower labels even when the lists of lengths everywhere consist only of ones.

In the subdivision-first family even the middle map may be taken to be a **single binary split or a single collapse**. Factor a longer subdivision as $f_1\odot f_2$ with $f_2$ elementary. Induct on the number of elementary factors of the first map, uniformly in the last simultaneous one-edge map $g$. Factor $f_2\odot g=\beta\odot h$ as before. The induction hypothesis gives $E(f_1,\beta)$, and (12) with last map $h$ gives $E(f_1, f_2\odot g)$ since both tests ending in $h$ hold. Use (12) on $f_1,f_2,g$, with $E(f_2,g)$ the elementary hypothesis, to conclude. Variable inclusions act by restriction and the identity has its disk square. This bounds the number of top edges that the middle map must change in that family independently of the branching and of $r$.

**Verification.** The checker takes raw substitutions, projects their position terms, recovers the arity and checks (10) entry by entry. Inputs include a source site supporting two target sites, an interval with no advance on either site, and a whole source string whose pole is supported one degree lower. Mixed and total collapses give identical recovered $\alpha$ in ranks two through four. Changing just the syntax of an intermediate pole changes $\alpha$ at that position with all lengths fixed. In a separate branching example the checker specializes one generic square $K$ at both the raw and the normalized block composite, computes the two promoted paths, and constructs the transported comparison (7) with those two specialized squares as factors. It checks the literal faces in degrees five and six. This last calculation uses top temporal marks, where the full mixed term can be checked on a globular graft. The deep-threshold proof uses the crossing of Response 23. Agda checked the total-collapse substitutions and the degree-five comparison in safe mode.
